### CONTENT ###
Chapter 1. The Evolution of Penetration Testing,
Red Teaming, and Bug Bounties
Chapter Objectives
After reading this chapter and completing the exercises, you will be able to
do the following:
• Understand the fundamental concepts and significance of penetration
testing, red teaming, and bug bounty programs
• Identify the different use cases for the individual programs in an
organization
• Develop strategies to effectively integrate the different programs into a
cohesive plan to improve the security posture of the organization
Into the Abyss
Let’s be honest; sometimes, being a red teamer can get a little predictable,
especially on an internal team. The organization has a robust bug bounty
program, and the external perimeter is well hardened. Sure, it's fun and still
a challenge at times. Sometimes, though, you want more, something that
will make you sweat a little, maybe second-guess career choices. That
something is what I (Wesley) got not too long ago.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p10_c006
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077

# TOKENS: ~340
### CONTENT ###
The objective for this red team engagement was to gain access to our
artificial intelligence/machine learning (AI/ML) research division and
attempt to taint training data, access proprietary information, and exfil
private datasets. I knew it would be a bit more of a learning curve than a
usual engagement, but I didn't know what I was in store for.
“Taint training data?” I scoffed to myself when I first saw the objective.
“That’s like trying to poison a unicorn’s dreams.” I figured it'd be another
walk in the park—do a little phishing, find a vulnerability, exploit it, move
laterally, game over. But, boy, was I wrong.
I think my arrogance got the best of me when I thought this would be quick
to set up. I had to get my head around AI vulnerabilities; a standard crash
course on a topic was not going to cut it. This was not going to be a
straightforward prompt injection, which wasn’t a strong suit either. I knew
it would be related to data poisoning and attacking the integrity of the
model. I immediately dug in and realized most training and research I was
finding were geared toward prompt engineering. Now, I am used to just-in-
time training (JITT) and the usual cycle of cramming in just enough to
accomplish the task before you move on to the next thing, but this was a
whole other thing. Who knew AI security couldn’t be learned in a few
hours?
### END CHUNK ###

The Uncharted Territory
While researching this objective, let’s also continue with the engagement.
Now, with a well-funded bug bounty program and regular penetration tests,
getting that initial foothold becomes increasingly difficult. Compounding
that was the fact that the research division didn’t have any public products,
there was zero attack surface, and our bug bounty program wouldn’t have
any insights for us.
Digging through open-source intelligence (OSINT) can be dull until you
find that piece of information that you pivot on and get what you were
looking for. In this case, it was public AI/ML platforms. We were blown
away by their level of activity on the platform, commenting on models and
reviewing datasets. A few patterns started to emerge, illustrating what they
were interested in. We determined that they were testing the models on their
laptops and in the cloud. We then noticed the organization was not
registered on the platform. Then it hit us; a textbook watering hole attack
with a sprinkling of organization confusion. It was a nice change of pace
from your typical spearphish.
We needed to balance the right amount of realism without replicating a
specific user who could be easily contacted. From our previous OSINT
work, we used some similar-sounding names to other employees at the
organization. We needed our model to just be convincing enough that this
person could manage the organization on the platform. We utilized an

With a created persona, then we would need to create a fake organization.
That was the easy part—just use the official name and logo. Without any
form of verification on the platform, we could be whoever we wanted to.
We stole some boilerplate text that we had seen in some of their GitHub
repos to make them feel at home.
The AI Crash Course
This scenario wouldn’t be your typical phishing payload, and we had to
pivot because our normal playbook wouldn't work. The clock was ticking,
and my understanding of AI vulnerabilities felt like a house of cards in a
hurricane. The pressure was immense. Each failed attempt to grasp the
nuances of adversarial attacks or model poisoning felt like a punch to the
gut. I was out of my depth, and the deadline loomed large.
In desperation, I reached out to a few data scientist friends, the kind who
were always tinkering with the latest AI models and frameworks. Their eyes
lit up when I explained my predicament. They saw it not as a crisis but as a
challenge, a puzzle to be solved.
Over countless cups of coffee and late-night video calls, they patiently
guided me through the labyrinth of AI security. They helped me build a
mental model, a framework where I could slot in the bits and pieces of
information I was frantically gathering. Their explanations were peppered

But theory alone wasn't going to cut it. I needed to get my hands dirty. So, I
turned to the AI oracle itself: ChatGPT. My search history from those days
is probably a hilarious (and slightly embarrassing) testament to my frantic
quest for knowledge. I bombarded the model with questions, ranging from
the basic to the esoteric, hoping to glean some practical wisdom.
Then came a breakthrough. A black hat talk and a couple of research papers
on embedding malicious code in models caught my eye. The Keras lambda
layer—a seemingly innocuous component that could be weaponized to
execute arbitrary code. It was like finding a hidden trapdoor in a seemingly
impenetrable fortress.
Fueled by my newfound knowledge and a surge of adrenaline, I dove
headfirst into experimentation. The lambda layer became my playground, a
canvas for crafting a simple Python stager that would download and execute
a Command and Control (C2) agent. It was a delicate dance, balancing
stealth with functionality.
With each successful test, my confidence grew. The fog of confusion lifted,
replaced by a sense of purpose and determination. I was no longer a red

The clock was still ticking, but the panic had subsided. I had the tools, the
knowledge, and the resolve to face the AI challenge head-on. The sprint to
the finish line had begun, and I was ready.
Into the Breach
We seeded the AI/ML platform with our newly created malicious model.
We added a little bit of window dressing to enhance our lure. The trap was
finally set, our malicious model mixed in with legitimate ones. Now the
waiting game began.
Then, it happened! A notification that a C2 agent beaconed back, and this
time we weren’t testing. Our model had been downloaded and executed.
The adrenaline surged through us, a mix of exhilaration and trepidation.
Our objective was clear: infiltrate the research division. So, instead of the
usual mad dash for domain admin, we took a more measured approach. We
meticulously enumerated the host, cataloging its secrets. We identified some
local data on the laptop, a potential treasure trove. There were numerous
sets of training data to go through. However, as we dug deeper, our hopes
plummeted. It was all synthetic or properly anonymized data. The
emotional rollercoaster of discovery and disappointment was exhausting.
### END CHUNK ###

We couldn't afford to dwell on setbacks. We went back to sifting through
the host's history. A pattern emerged: frequent interactions with another
internal host. We could tell that the user interacted with the host multiple
times a day. It was a host none of us were familiar with. It was a
breadcrumb trail we couldn't ignore.
The host was single sign-on (SSO) protected, but we had a trick up our
sleeve: session hijacking. We would still need their credentials though. So,
we crafted a fake pop-up, a convincing replica of the SSO login page, and
timed it to appear during the user's early morning routine. We adjusted our
C2 agent to beacon back to us in the morning before we kicked off the
attack. With our C2 agent lying dormant, waiting to spring to life, we were
ready to capture the precious credentials.
The plan worked flawlessly. Our C2 beaconed back and we were ready. The
pop-up captured the user’s credentials. We were ready for the next steps.
We extracted the cookies from Chrome; we now had everything we needed
for the internal site. A quick proxy setup and we were in. The site hosted
Jupyter notebooks, filled with lines of Python AI code. As I analyzed the
code, it quickly grew beyond my grasp of AI. My lack of AI knowledge was
a glaring weakness.
Thankfully, my data scientist friends had equipped me with a new skill:
prompt engineering. It was time to put that skill to the test. I carefully
crafted queries, beginning to understand not only the code, but the flow of

learned where it was located: a data source, non-anonymized customer data

Finally, we were in the last stages of the attack. We verified access controls
of the data source, and to our surprise, we determined that we could write to
the data source. Now if we were some nefarious attacker, we could just go
ahead and start manipulating the data; however, we wanted to ensure that
we would survive to run another engagement. During our initial queries of
the database, we determined that it was a cloud-hosted database. We got
lucky with some metadata that included the original query as the source of
the data. We knew then that this was a copy of data used for training and not
the original data. For training the model, it appeared that it was a one-time
snapshot of the data. They were not refreshing the data regularly. This
dataset consisted of competitor analysis and sales figures for our widgets.
The function of the model was to predict competitor pricing and suggest
pricing for our widgets.
With our lack of data analytic skills, the most straightforward attack we
thought of was reducing competitor pricing. This should result in
suggesting lower prices, thus reducing profitability. We wrote a rough
Python script to randomly reduce a competitor's price by 1–10 percent.
Now we had to wait for them to tweak and train the model. I wish I could
say it happened quickly, but we ended up waiting a week before they ran it

Culmination
Our objective was complete. We had breached the research division,
accessed sensitive data, and exposed vulnerabilities in their AI models. It
was a hard-fought victory, a testament to our team’s adaptability and
perseverance.
As we called it a day, a sense of satisfaction washed over us. We had not
only completed the engagement but also learned valuable lessons about the
ever-evolving landscape of AI security. This experience would undoubtedly
shape our future red team endeavors, reminding us that even in the face of
the unknown, with the right tools, knowledge, and a dash of ingenuity, we
can overcome any challenge.
Next Steps
This story is more than just an account of a successful red team
engagement. It's a glimpse into the unpredictable, ever-evolving world of
cybersecurity, where the stakes are high, and the challenges are relentless.
It's a world where adaptability, ingenuity, and a thirst for knowledge are not
just nice-to-haves but essential skills.

The goal of this book is to equip you with the tools and knowledge you
need to navigate this complex landscape with confidence. We want to take
you beyond the theoretical and into the trenches. We want to share the hard-
won lessons, battle scars, and strategies that have helped us overcome
seemingly insurmountable obstacles.
Whether you’re a seasoned red teamer or just starting your journey in
cybersecurity, this book is for you. We want to empower you to face the
unknown, embrace the challenges, and emerge victorious despite the odds
stacked against you. We’re going to show you that with the right mindset,
skills, and a little bit of grit, you can handle anything.
Industry Overview
Cybersecurity has evolved significantly from its early days and will
continue to do so. The history of cybersecurity mirrors the rise of
computing itself. In the early days of computing, security was not a priority,
the focus was primarily on functionality. The first controls evolved out of
the need to share a limited computing resource among users, not from any
inherent concern for malicious actors. Soon after these controls were put in
place, there were individuals who were seeking to exploit their weaknesses
to gain additional resources. The cat-and-mouse game of protection and
exploitation began, driving the evolution of the cybersecurity field it is
today. Computers have become more pervasive in our lives, and the sheer
volume of sensitive data that they process and store has exploded. The need
### END CHUNK ###

to protect this has evolved into specific roles focused on different aspects of
security.
An organization with a mature cybersecurity program will focus on three
core pillars, each fulfilling a unique but interconnected function.
Penetration testing, red teaming, and bug bounty programs form this triad of
modern cybersecurity. While each of these programs serves a distinct
purpose, their effectiveness is amplified when they are implemented in
concert. Each individual program will increase the effectiveness of the
others when they inform each other. Providing an ongoing feedback loop
generated between them fosters continuous improvement, enabling
organizations to stay one step ahead of evolving threats.
What Are Penetration Testing, Red Teaming, and Bug Bounties?
As cybersecurity has matured, specific disciplines have emerged over the
years. These essential practices of penetration testing, red teaming, and bug
bounties evolved to address the threat landscape and play a crucial role in
bolstering organizational security. These different disciplines are often
confused with each other, so it is important to understand the terms as well
as the differences. We will dive deeper into these in the following sections,
but for now, we will define them. Penetration testing, often referred to as
pen testing, is a security assessment designed to identify vulnerabilities and
verify them through exploitation.
### END CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
Why Are These Disciplines Important?
In today’s interconnected world, where cyber threats are in the news every
single day, organizations are facing increasingly sophisticated and persistent
threats. These organizations need to take a proactive and holistic approach
to cybersecurity. Penetration testing, bug bounties, and red teaming are no
longer optional activities; they are essential components of a robust
cybersecurity strategy. These components offer distinct yet complementary
approaches to assessing defenses, uncovering vulnerabilities, and promoting
continuous improvement in security posture. Each approach has its unique
advantages and disadvantages. Penetration testing provides a structured and
systematic way to proactively uncover vulnerabilities. Red teaming
emulates real-world adversaries and their attacks, allowing organizations
the opportunity to test their defenses against highly motivated and skilled
adversaries. Bug bounties leverage the skills of the community to identify
issues that might not normally be identified. These disciplines, together,
provide a multi-layered approach to ensure that organizations stay one step
ahead of malicious actors.
How Have They Evolved Over Time?
The disciplines of penetration testing, bug bounties, and red teaming have
undergone significant transformations over the years in response to the
ever-evolving cyber threat landscape. It was not until recent times that we
saw them come together in a cohesive approach. In the early days of
### END CHUNK ###

penetration testing, it was a very manual process to identify vulnerabilities.
Information was not shared as readily and neither were tools available. As
the technology stack has advanced, so have methodologies and tooling
availability.
Similarly, red teaming has evolved from military-related “war gaming”
exercises to highly sophisticated long-term engagements that emulate the
tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs) of real-world threat actors and
their attacks. Just as adversaries have grown in sophistication, so have
operators. Most operations emulate advanced social engineering and
malware development to assess an organization’s defenses.
Finally, bug bounties have also gone through a maturation process. What
started off as informal arrangements with limited scope and unknown
rewards have become well-established programs. These days, many
organizations have structured programs with substantial financial incentives
for researchers to participate in programs. They leverage the collective
expertise of the community to identify issues.
The Early Days and Evolution of Penetration Testing
During these early days of penetration testing, testing wasn’t as formal
when compared to the sophisticated assessments performed today. They are
often in response to “How secure is the system?” Similar to red teaming
having its roots in the military, so does penetration testing. These tests were
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p22_c018
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077

### CONTENT ###
performed by a Tiger Team, a team of individuals with specialized skills to
address a specific problem. This term was originally coined by Walter C.
Williams, an engineer at the Manned Spacecraft Center, in the 1964 paper
titled “Program Management in Design and Development.” This was then
further expounded on by James P. Anderson in the 1972 report “Computer
Security Technology Planning Study”
(https://csrc.nist.rip/publications/history/ande72a.pdf).
These early penetration tests and methodology laid the groundwork for
penetration tests today. They brought to light principles that are used every
day in penetration tests. This effort was crucial in helping organizations
realize the importance of proactive security assessments. As penetration
tests evolved, they formalized processes, frameworks, and tooling.
In the early days of penetration testing, the development of methodologies
and tooling was a very organic process, often happening in silos. This work
was driven mainly by the ingenuity and experience of these early
individuals. These initial penetration tests were very ad hoc approaches,
relying heavily on the individual skills and knowledge of the testers. There
was a lack of standardized methodology, with a focus on improvisation and
creativity to achieve success. Anderson’s framework, which he outlined in
his “Computer Security Technology Planning Study,” paved the way for
future frameworks to follow (see Figure 1-1). Helping to standardize the
methodology, Penetration Testing Execution Standard (PTES) and Open
Web Application Security Project (OWASP) Testing guide emerged. These

Figure 1-1 Computer Security Technology Planning Study
The evolution of tooling within penetration tests mirrored that of the
frameworks. In the early days, penetration testers relied heavily on manual
techniques and command-line tools. These tools required a deep
understanding of operating systems, network protocols, and programming
languages. These efforts evolved into custom scripting as testers sought to
automate specific tasks. This automation allowed them to increase in
velocity and share among team members. Some of these tools would evolve
into vulnerability scanners because they were packaged together, reducing
the time and effort required for manual checks. As collaboration beyond
teams grew, many of these tools would become open-sourced. This open
access was a boon for the community and played a crucial role in their rapid
development and expansion.
The development of penetration testing methodologies and tools was a
continuous process of innovation and refinement. Early pioneers paved the
way for the sophisticated and structured approaches that are used today.
They demonstrate the importance of proactive security assessment in an
increasingly interconnected world.
The Rise of Red Teaming
Red teaming has its roots in the early days of military war games. In the
1960s the United States military began using war games to test the
effectiveness of its operational plans. This initial set of exercises was called
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p26_c021

the Sigma War Games and was carried out in the Pentagon. A group of 40–
50 experts would gather to prepare for each exercise. These individuals
would develop the scenario based on current global conditions and predict
future events. For the execution phase of the exercise, about 30 people
would participate. This phase consisted of two teams: the Red Team, who
played the role of the enemy forces, and the Blue Team, the friendly forces.
A third group called Control, sometimes referred to as White Cell, would
oversee the exercise. Their role during the exercise was to monitor for
adherence to the rules of engagement (ROEs) and coordination of activities
to achieve exercise goals.
Since these early days of exercises, the term red teaming has been applied
to a wider variety of activities. In the 1980s the concept began to be applied
to cybersecurity. Many early engagements still required physical access to
these systems. As ARPANET gave way to the public Internet and systems
became more interconnected, the engagements transitioned to more remote
attacks. The National Security Agency led the way in this new digital
landscape. The NSA transitioned from its traditional signals and
cryptographic focus to a cybersecurity focus.
Red team engagements have evolved significantly over the last 30 or so
years. In the earliest of red team engagements, that target was typically
military or government computer systems. These early engagements lasted
only a few days or weeks. Within the military, red teaming has become a
specialized position requiring specific training. These dedicated teams of

chapter. As the threat landscape has evolved with adversaries executing
increasingly sophisticated attacks, red teams have to emulate these attacks.
This increased complexity has caused the length of many of these
engagements to increase from weeks to months. Engagements will also
encompass a wider array of assessments. These engagements will cover
physical and social engineering as well as technical assessments.
Organizations recognize the need for full-spectrum engagements.
The Emergence of Bug Bounty Programs
With the popularity of bug bounty programs in the last few years, it is hard
to believe that they can be traced back to the early days of computing. In
1983 a small software company named Hunter & Ready is credited with
launching the first documented bug bounty program. The company was
advertising its new Versatile Real-Time Executive (VRTX) operating
system as being bug-free. The VRTX was designed for real-time
applications, where even minor bugs would have a significant impact. The
reliability of the operating system was crucial for the company’s reputation.
Hunter & Ready was so confident in it that the company published a full-
page ad with the slogan “Get a bug if you find a bug.” It offered a
Volkswagen Beetle or $1,000 in cash to anyone who could find one and
### END CHUNK ###

uniqueness of the advertising but also for the award of a “Bug for a bug.”
This program demonstrated an early understanding that external
perspectives could be invaluable in enhancing software security. It
foreshadowed the collaborative approach that would become a cornerstone
of modern bug bounty programs.
A little over a decade later, Netscape launched a more formal bug bounty
program that most closely resembles something we see today. In 1995, the
company launched a “Bugs Bounty” initiative for the Netscape Navigator
2.0 browser. This program marked a turning point for the industry and is
recognized as the first formal bug bounty program. The program focused on
cash rewards and responsible disclosure of the bugs. This approach
incentivized ethical hackers and security researchers to actively search for
vulnerabilities within a specific product. This program created a symbiotic
relationship between the company and the community. It created a shift in
perspectives. Instead of relying solely on internal testing, Netscape
embraced external expertise to enhance its security posture. It was a
successful program, even though it was short-lived. It ran until the final
release of Netscape Navigator 2.0. It helped uncover several vulnerabilities
and improved the overall security of the browser. The program also
provided lessons learned for future programs. The limited scope and
duration of the program reduced its overall impact. Future programs would
learn from this and establish enduring efforts with much broader scopes. It

While these early programs were groundbreaking, the bug bounty landscape
didn’t immediately explode. These pioneering efforts set the stage for the
next era of bug bounty. It took a number of years and key developments to
truly establish bug bounty programs as standard practice. Yet, their legacy
continues to inspire organizations to leverage the expertise of the security
research community to enhance their security posture.
Building on the groundwork laid by the early programs, Mozilla launched
its vulnerability disclosure program in 2004. Most known for its open-
source Firefox browser, the program established new norms within the
community. The company offered an open-invite program for security
researchers like earlier programs but differed with its transparency and
recognition. Mozilla pioneered a policy of transparency and recognition by
publicly crediting researchers who responsibly disclosed vulnerabilities.
This approach helped establish a sense of community and encouraged
ethical reporting practices. The public recognition helped the community
grow by identifying other researchers and encouraging collaboration. As
with previous programs, the company offered financial rewards for
disclosing vulnerabilities. It established the practice of different rewards
based on the severity of the vulnerability. Like previous programs that
focused on a single product, the company evolved its scope from solely the
Firefox browser to include its other products as well. During this time,

network of ethical hackers, facilitating the identification and remediation of
vulnerabilities before they can be exploited by malicious actors.

of cybersecurity and the crucial role that collaboration between

the digital world.
The Convergence of Penetration Testing, Red Teaming, and
Bug Bounties
Through penetration testing, red teaming, and bug bounty programs, an
organization has one common goal—to improve its security posture. Using
these proactive measures to identify and address these vulnerabilities
reduces risk across the organization. A number of factors drive
organizations. An evolving threat landscape with the sophistication of cyber
attacks has escalated quickly. Organizations require a more holistic and
proactive security strategy to stay ahead of evolving threats. Point-in-time
security assessments are no longer sufficient. Organizations need ongoing

Hybrid engagements represent a significant advancement in the field of
security testing. By strategically combining diverse assessment
methodologies and leveraging both internal and external expertise,
organizations can achieve a more comprehensive and effective security
posture. As the threat landscape continues to evolve, hybrid engagements
will likely become even more prevalent, enabling organizations to
proactively defend against increasingly sophisticated cyber attacks.
The convergence of these security practices represents a positive shift
toward a more comprehensive and adaptable approach to safeguarding
organizations in an increasingly complex cyber landscape. The ability to
leverage the results of penetration testing, red team engagements, and bug
bounty programs to inform and plan future efforts is indispensable. By
harnessing the strengths of each discipline and fostering collaboration,
organizations can build a robust and resilient security posture.

Penetration testing, red teaming, and bug bounties are the cornerstones of
proactive defense. Once considered isolated activities, their boundaries are
blurring as security teams embrace a unified approach to identifying
vulnerabilities. Penetration testing is a tried-and-true method that simulates
real-world attacks to uncover weaknesses. Tomorrow's penetration testers

The Increasing Use of AI and ML in Penetration Testing, Red
Teaming, and Bug Bounties
The integration of artificial intelligence and machine learning is ushering in
a new era of cybersecurity practices. There will be a divide between
security professionals and organizations of those who evolve with it and
those who do not. It is becoming an expectation that all roles have some
level of familiarity with artificial intelligence and machine learning. There
are many different avenues to integrate into workflows, and we will discuss
a few within this book.
In penetration testing, AI-powered tools will automate information
gathering and reconnaissance, rapidly identifying potential targets,
vulnerabilities, and attack paths, thus enabling penetration testers to focus
on strategic exploitation. AI-driven models will analyze attack surfaces and
potential threat actors, guiding penetration testers to prioritize the most
critical areas of vulnerability. ML algorithms can be utilized to analyze
vulnerability severity, likelihood of exploitation, and potential impact, and
as a result, greatly reducing the amount of time penetration testers are
reviewing results, and providing prioritized targets. Lastly, a personal
favorite is the ability to generate detailed reporting based on results with
remediation recommendations.
The ability of AI models to analyze and provide predictive threat modeling
allows red teamers to focus on the advanced techniques and proactively

The increasing use of AI and ML is poised to revolutionize penetration
testing, red teaming, and bug bounties, driving innovation and improving

### CONTENT ###
Chapter 2. Introduction to Red Teaming
Chapter Objectives
After reading this chapter and completing the exercises, you will be able to
do the following:
• Understand what red teaming is and its importance
• Identify common red team frameworks and methodologies
• Explain types of red team engagements
• Understand the challenges that red teams may face
What Is Red Teaming?
In the preceding chapter, we dug into the history of red teaming and its
roots in 1960s military war games. Then we followed along as it evolved
into modern-day red teaming. Red teaming can take on a variety of forms
because it can be applied in a variety of roles and even outside of
cybersecurity.
Let’s take a moment to outline what red teaming is. It is a broader, multi-
layered approach that encompasses a wide variety of tactics, techniques,
and procedures (TTPs). It utilizes not only TTPs from real-world
adversaries to test technical security controls but also the human element,
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p43_c037
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 43
# TOKENS: ~335
### CONTENT ###
processes, and the overall security posture of an organization. Red teaming
offers an adversarial perspective essential for testing security assumptions
and evaluating the effectiveness of the people, processes, and technologies
employed to defend an environment.
Any offensive security testing, such as a penetration test, is often
misinterpreted as red teaming. They both play important roles in
cybersecurity, but they tackle different goals and use different approaches.
The goal of a penetration test is to find as many vulnerabilities as possible
in a controlled manner, typically within a defined scope and timeframe. The
objective is solely to exploit the system(s) and document weaknesses for
remediation. It does not challenge the detection and response capabilities,
nor does it evaluate the involvement of people or processes in preventing
such attacks.
Red teaming generally focuses on achieving a particular objective, such as
gaining access to sensitive data or compromising critical infrastructure. It is
designed to challenge the detection and response capabilities of an
organization to assess how well they can identify, respond, and contain an
ongoing attack over an extended period from persistent threats. The
emphasis here is on creatively executing multi-step attacks to achieve the
objective while avoiding detection.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p44_c038

# TOKENS: ~348
### CONTENT ###
Red teaming comes in many different forms, and we will dive into the wide
variety of engagements later in this chapter. However, let’s discuss the
importance of red teaming first.
The Importance of Red Teaming
Red teaming is an essential component of a robust cybersecurity strategy,
offering a proactive approach to identifying and addressing weaknesses in
an organization’s defenses. By emulating real-world adversarial tactics,
techniques, and procedures, red teaming provides unparalleled insights into
an organization’s resilience against cyber threats. Its importance can be
broken down into several key aspects: identifying critical vulnerabilities,
challenging assumptions, and emulating industry-specific relevant threats.
Identifying Critical Vulnerabilities
Red team engagements are uniquely designed to uncover critical
vulnerabilities by adopting a holistic and multi-dimensional approach to
security testing. Unlike narrowly focused penetration tests that typically
target specific systems or applications, red teaming evaluates an
organization’s entire security ecosystem. This comprehensive methodology
ensures that no critical weak points are overlooked, providing actionable
insights into the organization’s overall risk posture.
Red teaming focuses on targeted and opportunistic real-world attacks to
expose critical vulnerabilities. Unlike comprehensive penetration testing,
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p45_c039

which aims for complete coverage, red teaming strategically probes for
weaknesses that could have the most significant impact. This approach
prioritizes remediation efforts by identifying and exploiting interconnected
vulnerabilities across physical, technical, and human domains. For example,
a successful red team exercise might demonstrate how a social engineering
attack, combined with a physical security lapse, could lead to a critical
system compromise, even if individual technical vulnerabilities are patched.
Holistic red teaming ensures that security measures are not only robust in
isolation but also resilient when tested against complex, multi-vector
attacks. This level of insight is invaluable for organizations striving to
defend against sophisticated adversaries and continuously improve their
security defenses.
Challenging Assumptions
Another significant benefit of red teaming is its ability to challenge existing
assumptions about an organization’s security posture by adopting an
adversarial perspective. Security measures are often designed and
implemented based on theoretical scenarios, compliance requirements, or
past experiences. While these approaches provide a foundation, they may
overlook evolving threats, misjudge the ingenuity of real attackers, or rely
too heavily on untested assumptions.
By simulating real-world adversarial behavior, red teams put these
assumptions to the test, identifying gaps or weaknesses that would
### END CHUNK ###

multifactor authentication (MFA) is sufficient to secure sensitive accounts.
A red team, however, might exploit social engineering tactics to bypass

codes. These exercises highlight potential flaws in processes that, while
theoretically robust, may fail under real-world conditions.
This validation is critical for ensuring that security controls perform as
expected, not only in controlled environments but also under the stress of
actual attacks. It shifts the focus from the dreaded "checkbox security" of
meeting regulatory or compliance requirements to actually building security
systems that are truly resilient against modern threats.
Another crucial aspect in challenging the assumption after validating the
security control is understanding the impact. Whereas most traditional
pentests would stop at phishing or acquiring the codes, a red team
engagement shows the impact of those actions. What could an adversary
accomplish with those MFA tokens? Does this role provide access to source
code or core infrastructure? The longer duration engagements and the
knowledge of the environment give the red team the advantage to test the
assumptions of the controls and illustrate the impact once bypassed. This
improved understanding of the impact can better illustrate the risk—thus
providing a holistic view rather than a single vulnerability.

Ultimately, this adversarial perspective promotes a more adaptive and
realistic approach to security. It encourages organizations to constantly

threats, making their security posture more robust and resilient.
Emulating Industry-Specific Threats
The specific threats an organization will likely face can vary depending on
the industry. For example, a financial organization is often targeted by
highly specialized groups, such as those involved in banking fraud or
targeting payment systems. In contrast, healthcare organizations often face
threats from ransomware groups seeking to disrupt critical systems or steal
patient data. A tailored red team engagement might involve simulating
ransomware propagation or exfiltration of electronic health records, testing
not just the technical defenses but also the response protocols and business
continuity plans. The exact TTPs are replicated in the red team
engagements to test the relevant defenses that the organization is most
likely to encounter. By replicating the exact threats an organization is likely
to encounter, red teaming ensures that security measures are aligned with
the realities of the threat landscape.
Red teaming provides an opportunity for organizations to gain insight into
how effectively they can detect, respond to, and recover from real-life cyber
attacks. The emulation of adversarial attacks gives the security operations
center (SOC) the ability to evaluate current detections and alerts. Creating

Red teaming is a powerful tool for organizations to strengthen their security
posture proactively. It provides a real-world lens through which to evaluate
vulnerabilities, challenge assumptions, and test defenses comprehensively.
By tailoring engagements to industry-specific threats and focusing on
detection, response, and recovery, red teaming enables organizations to
anticipate and mitigate risks effectively, fostering resilience in an ever-
evolving threat landscape.
Red Team Frameworks and Methodologies
In the ever-evolving landscape of cybersecurity, red teams play a crucial
role in proactively identifying and mitigating vulnerabilities before
malicious actors exploit them. To effectively emulate real-world adversaries
and provide meaningful assessments, red teams rely on established
frameworks that guide their operations and enhance communication with
clients. These frameworks offer a standardized approach to understanding
adversary TTPs, enabling red teams to conduct more realistic and targeted
attacks. Moreover, they bridge the gap between offensive and defensive
security perspectives by providing a common language for discussing
threats and vulnerabilities. This shared understanding facilitates
collaboration between red teams, blue teams, and leadership within an
organization. This collaboration ultimately results in more effective
### END CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
detection engineering and overall improvement in an organization’s security

The sections that follow describe the frameworks that represent some of the
most common and valuable resources used by red teams today.
MITRE ATT&CK Framework
The MITRE ATT&CK Framework is a comprehensive knowledge base that
systematically catalogs adversary TTPs observed across the kill chain. It is
recognized across the industry and is a standard for effectively
communicating about cyber attacks. It was developed by the MITRE
corporation and is regularly updated to reflect the evolving threat landscape.
It is utilized across organizations, such as the red team, threat intel, and
SOC teams, to ensure they understand the risk and address it.
The ATT&CK Framework is organized into a number of matrices, each one
focusing on a different domain. They are tailored for each of the domains:
Enterprise, ICS, and Mobile. Red teams leverage the framework to emulate
real-world adversary behavior during engagements. It also aids in mapping
red team activities to specific TTPs, which facilitates the detailed analysis
of detection engineering and the measurement of security control
effectiveness.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p50_c044
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 50
# TOKENS: ~355
### CONTENT ###
Unified Kill Chain
The Unified Kill Chain (UKC) is a conceptual framework that is the
evolution of a number of previous efforts. It addresses the limitations of
previous models through the integration of their strengths in a unified
approach. It is designed to provide a holistic view of adversary behavior
across the entire attack lifecycle.
Unlike the MITRE ATT&CK Framework, which we previously mentioned,
the Unified Kill Chain focuses on both the technical and strategic aspects of
cyber attacks. It incorporates techniques from initial compromise,
persistence, lateral movement, and impact while aligning with broader risk
management and operational objectives. It focuses on end-to-end coverage
of the attack. It ties specific technical actions to strategic goals for the
attacker. This allows an organization to understand how these TTPs
contribute to the broader adversary goals in isolation. The flexibility of the
kill chain is that it complements other frameworks like MITRE ATT&CK,
providing a unified narrative of adversarial campaigns. Lastly, it is utilized
by red teams to emulate sophisticated attack sequences, helping
organizations test their security controls in a holistic manner.
TIBER-EU
Threat Intelligence-Based Ethical Red Teaming (TIBER-EU) is a
framework developed by the European Central Bank (ECB) for financial
institutions in the European Union that provide core financial infrastructure.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p51_c045

The main emphasis is on the use of threat intelligence to tailor exercises to
specific threats faced by an organization. The other focus of the framework
is to provide a consistent and comprehensive approach to red teaming,
ensuring that exercises are conducted to a high standard. This approach
enables the results to be comparable across institutions. The result of the
exercise is focused on increasing the cyber maturity and resilience of the
organization; this is done through identifying the strengths and weaknesses
of the financial institution. It is common to utilize this framework if you are
based in the EU. For example, a major European bank wants to assess its
resilience against sophisticated APT groups targeting SWIFT infrastructure.
This is the type of framework that you will be required to utilize. The
participants include five teams in an exercise:
• Threat Intelligence: A third-party team that performs reconnaissance and
analysis of the financial institution. This team provides a comprehensive list
of possible threat actors and their TTPs.
• Blue Team: The financial institution’s internal team, who are responsible
for the prevention, detection, and response capabilities.
• Red Team: A third-party team that carries out the emulated attack against
the financial institution.
• White Team: Trusted agents internal to the financial institution.

CBEST
The Bank of England's Cyber Assessment Framework (CBEST) is another
intelligence-led approach to red teaming. It is a comprehensive framework
used to assess and enhance the cyber resilience of UK financial institutions.
Red team activities are tailored to specific threats and vulnerabilities that
are faced by the specific financial institution that is being assessed. It
prioritizes the testing of critical systems and functions that are essential for
the institution's operations. The assessment follows the principle of the
“golden thread,” which is a traceable link between the identified threats

Red Team Engagements
Red team engagements come in various forms, each tailored to specific
objectives and organizational needs. Think of it like the right tool for the
job. Choosing a hammer to turn a screw may not be the optimal outcome or
effective use of resources. Similarly, different cybersecurity threats require
different approaches to testing your defenses. The choice of engagement
depends on a variety of factors, including the organization’s size and
industry, its risk tolerance, and the desired outcomes it hopes to achieve.
For example, a small startup will have different needs and risks when
compared to a multinational bank. Similarly, an organization primarily
concerned with data breaches will require different types of red team
engagements than one focused on physical security vulnerabilities.
Understanding the different types of red team engagements is crucial for
selecting the most appropriate approach to assess and enhance an
organization's security posture. Let's explore some of the most common
types of red team engagements.
Full-Scope
A full-scope engagement is the classic and most extensive type of
engagement. The red team utilizes everything within its arsenal,
incorporating threat intelligence, physical and social engineering, as well as
cyber, usually with an extended timeline to accomplish these activities. This
### END CHUNK ###

resilience. During a full-scope engagement, a significant investment is
required from a time and resource perspective. Depending on the size of the
organization, this type of engagement may be too resource intensive to
accomplish regularly.

intellectual property), to manipulate and/or exfiltrate the data. These
objective-based engagements typically have a defined start and end. Due to

Adversary Emulation (Scenario-Based)
In adversary emulation engagements, the focus is on simulating a realistic,
end-to-end attack scenario that mimics the tactics, techniques, and
procedures of a specific threat actor or type of adversary. Unlike objective-
based engagements that have a narrowly defined goal, adversary emulation-
based engagements take a broader approach, replicating the lifecycle of an
attack from initial reconnaissance to achieving multiple potential outcomes.
The scenarios are designed to represent real-world attack conditions,
emphasizing the organization’s ability to defend against, detect, and
respond to evolving threats. These engagements are often rooted in specific
use cases, such as advanced persistent threat (APT) simulations, supply
chain attacks, or ransomware incidents, and can range from a few weeks to
several months, depending on the complexity and scope. The use of threat
intelligence is critical in determining the relevant threat actor and TTPs to
emulate.
Table 2-3 outlines the benefits and drawbacks of adversary emulation
(scenario-based) engagements.

These types of exercises involve a collaborative approach where both the
red team and blue team work together to simulate and respond to attacks in
real time. The goal is mutual learning and continuous improvement rather
than just adversarial exercises. The red team demonstrates and executes
emulated attacks, while the blue team actively detects, responds to, and
mitigates these attacks. This engagement fosters open communication and
knowledge sharing, allowing both teams to understand each other’s
perspectives and enhance the organization’s overall security posture.

Tabletop
Tabletop exercises (TTXs) are structured, discussion-based security
simulations designed to evaluate an organization’s readiness for handling
various scenarios. These scenarios can take on a wide variety of types, from
cyber attacks to natural disasters or other crises. Unlike live simulations,
TTXs are conducted in a controlled environment where participants discuss
actions and responses to hypothetical incidents without actively deploying
tools or systems. These exercises are typically facilitated by a moderator
who guides the scenario, posing challenges and prompting participants to
consider their response strategies. TTXs focus on testing policies,
### END CHUNK ###

red team may participate in or facilitate; however, it is something that is

Red Team Challenges
There are a number of challenges that red teamers will face during their
career. While they don’t happen often, it is important to understand and
prepare for them when they do arise. The following sections explore some
of the common challenges faced by red teams, from navigating complex
organizational structures to dealing with the frustration of “no-findings”
engagements. By acknowledging these hurdles and developing strategies to
overcome them, red teams can consistently improve their effectiveness and
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p63_c056

contribute to a stronger security posture for the organization. Let’s explore
some of those challenges that red teams may face.
Leveling Up Other Teams
An often overlooked aspect of red teaming is delivering value to other
teams. It’s not just about identifying vulnerabilities and doing cool things;
the true value is that the security knowledge across the organization has
risen. A mature red team can contribute to other teams in numerous ways.
A red team can significantly boost the blue team’s capabilities by providing
them with realistic attack scenarios and insights into adversary mindsets.
This practical experience allows the blue team to improve their detection
and response mechanisms, fine-tune security tools, and strengthen their
overall incident response capabilities. Essentially, the red team acts as a
sparring partner, helping the blue team to train and prepare for real-world
threats.
The red team’s findings provide invaluable feedback for the threat
intelligence team. Through sharing information on exploited vulnerabilities,
attack vectors, and observed tactics, techniques, and procedures, the red
team helps the threat intelligence team refine their threat models, improve
threat-hunting efforts, and provide more accurate and timely warnings
within the organization. This collaborative loop ensures that the
organization’s understanding of the threat landscape is continuously
updated and aligned with the latest threats.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p64_c057
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077

# TOKENS: ~371
### CONTENT ###
Red team engagements can be leveraged to create compelling security
awareness training for employees. By demonstrating real-world attack
scenarios and their potential impact, the red team can educate users about
common threats like phishing, social engineering, and malware. This
practical approach helps employees understand the importance of security
protocols and encourages them to adopt secure behaviors, ultimately
reducing the organization’s vulnerability. This approach can take on many
forms from something as simple as a “brown bag lunch demo” to a
presentation during an internal conference. A more hands-on and focused
approach will be discussed in the next paragraph.
By collaborating with engineering teams, red teams can provide insights
into secure coding practices and design considerations. This effort can
involve vulnerability assessments of new applications and threat modeling
during the development lifecycle. This is not the red team performing this
function but providing expertise if it is necessary. Nor does this replace
formal training in these specialties, but it provides just-in-time and
proactive guidance should the need arise. An example would be the
identification of inconsistent user input sanitization in various portions of
products. The red team can collaborate with engineers to identify the best
practices that are relevant to the organization and then work to disseminate
that information in the appropriate manner with the relevant engineers.
### END CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
Lack of Findings
Not every engagement is going to be perfect or full of the latest
vulnerabilities. There will be times when it may seem like a failure. Not
achieving your objective is just as informative. You will have performed a
validation of existing security controls. It proves that they have been
configured and implemented correctly, but maybe this is a surprise for you.
In the last couple of engagements, you just steamrolled through them;
however, this time, they closed out that Jira ticket for tweaking the control.
There may have been an implementation of a new security control that the
team was not tracking. This process provides an opportunity for the team to
learn and experiment with security control. Finally, it provides guidance for
future engagements. When the team is planning the next set of
engagements, there is an opportunity to refine the scope, re-evaluate risk,
and define new objectives. It is an opportunity to learn and improve, not a
sign of defeat.
This chapter introduces red teaming, highlighting its significance in
cybersecurity and contrasting it with penetration testing. While penetration
tests focus on uncovering vulnerabilities in a controlled scope, red teaming
adopts a broader, adversarial approach to assess an organization’s overall
resilience, including people, processes, and technology.
We then discussed the importance of red teaming in identifying critical
vulnerabilities, challenging security assumptions, and simulating industry-

specific threats. The chapter underscores how adversarial perspectives

We also explored key frameworks and methodologies, including

• Unified Kill Chain: A comprehensive view of attack lifecycles tied to

• Full-Scope: Holistic assessments involving multiple attack vectors
• Objective-Based: Assessments focused on achieving specific, measurable
goals
• Adversary Emulation: Simulations of realistic, end-to-end attack

• Purple Teaming: Collaborative exercises between red and blue teams for

### CONTENT ###
Chapter 3. Red Team Infrastructure
Chapter Objectives
After reading this chapter and completing the exercises, you will be able to
do the following:
• Understand common C2s
• Use C2 communication channels
• Use C2 redirectors
• Secure your infrastructure
• Understand how to set up C2 infrastructure
To begin setting up initial access, a red team infrastructure needs to be
created. The infrastructure being used for a red team needs to blend in like
legitimate traffic. Attention to detail during this step is critical because your
infrastructure as an attacker is what sets you up for success while executing
and maintaining access in a compromised environment.
In the last few years, there has been a significant shift toward using cloud
platforms such as Azure and Google Cloud for red team infrastructure.
Traditionally, operators would set up Command and Control (C2)
infrastructure on self-hosted servers, but the move to cloud services allows
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p73_c066
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 73
# TOKENS: ~369
### CONTENT ###
for more flexible, scalable, and resilient infrastructures. The ability to
dynamically spin up new instances or scale services based on the size of an
engagement has reduced the operational overhead.
Additionally, cloud services often integrate with content delivery networks
(CDNs), which can help blend C2 traffic with legitimate cloud provider
traffic, adding an additional layer of obfuscation. CDNs such as Cloudflare
or Azure’s Classic CDN are increasingly used to route C2 traffic through
legitimate and trusted web infrastructure. Red teamers can abuse CDNs by
blending in their traffic using these providers. This technique makes it more
difficult for incident response teams to distinguish malicious traffic from
legitimate traffic.
Another noticeable shift has been around the adoption of DNS over HTTPS
for red teamers’ C2 communication channels. These communications send
DNS queries over HTTPS (DoH) connections, thus bypassing traditional
network monitoring systems. It is critical to note that while DoH provides
obfuscation, it also introduces challenges in ensuring stable and reliable
communication because some corporate networks might block nonstandard
DNS requests. Additionally, older C2 frameworks leveraged static callback
intervals that could be detected by network monitoring systems. The
introduction of jitter in C2 profiles opened up the door for red teamers to
randomize their traffic associated with their agents and avoid predictable
intervals.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p74_c067
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 74
# TOKENS: ~360
### CONTENT ###
The purpose of this chapter is to review how development of infrastructure
has shifted in the past few years, reviewing common C2 communication
channels, and how you can set up your infrastructure for success. When
you’re preparing your red team infrastructure, you might want to set up
some items versus other items that may not be needed, depending on the
objective of your engagement. Red teams can operate from an assumed
breach standpoint or start from the perimeter, which requires targeting an
external application or user. We will dive deeper into the methodology side
of red teaming in Chapter 4, “Modern Red Teaming Methodology and
Tools.” Additionally, if social engineering is involved, you will need to
prepare phishing infrastructure or vishing infrastructure, which we will
discuss in Chapter 5, “Social Engineering and Physical Assessments.”
An Overview of Red Team Infrastructure
In years past, red team infrastructure for Command and Control
communications didn’t need to be as sophisticated as needed today;
however, there has been a shift in recent years. For example, leveraging
CDNs makes red teamers’ traffic appear to be from a legitimate company
such as Microsoft or other cloud providers. The traffic from major cloud
providers is typically trusted because real companies utilize CDNs for their
infrastructure. This technique circumvents traffic filters a company might
have in place on the perimeter if using CDNs.
### END CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
The infrastructure for a red team is the heart of your engagement and how
your operation comes together as a whole. Different elements should be
considered when building your infrastructure, including what is required for
meeting your objective, securing your infrastructure, having backup
infrastructure in place, and ensuring proper logging throughout the
engagement lifecycle for reporting purposes.
Command and Control
Command and Control servers are what operators use to manage their
operation to remotely perform actions on victims’ computers. These servers
are dependent on the C2 type being used because there are tons of
commercial and open-source options. The C2 matrix is an available open-
source document that professionals in the field can reference to understand
every C2 available from a commercial and public perspective with
information on agent types, socks support, and so on. The information is
located at https://howto.thec2matrix.com.
The top C2 options in the field from a commercial standpoint are
Nighthawk by MDSec and Cobalt Strike by Forta. The most common
publicly available C2 frameworks supported by the community are Mythic,
Sliver, Havoc, and BadRats. An advantage of public C2 frameworks is the
flexibility for customization to be most effective, whereas commercial C2s
can be more restrictive in terms of customization. Commercial C2s are still
### END CHUNK ###

Nighthawk (https://www.mdsec.co.uk/nighthawk/) was released in 2021
and is the latest advanced tradecraft C2 framework; it is intended for use
only by authorized red team operators. The MDSec team built the C2 by
leveraging custom research and development, which empowers the
advanced capabilities of the C2. The C2 supports multiple operators,
advanced evasion techniques, custom loaders, malleable profiles, SOCKS5,
Beacon Object Files (BOFs), covert communications, secure post
exploitation options, and a custom PE generator for operators.
Cobalt Strike (https://www.cobaltstrike.com/) was originally created by
Raphael Mudge in 2012 as a commercially available C2 for operators to
leverage during red team operations. Cobalt Strike comes with an Arsenal
Kit that is a tool suite of custom tools to improve your operator capabilities
for a successful engagement; it includes a sleep mask kit, custom loaders,
and other options to choose from. Cobalt Strike is widely used by
cybersecurity firms in the community for red team operations. The C2 also
supports multiple operators, BOFs, malleable profiles, SOCKS4/SOCKS5,
and covert communications.
Mythic (https://docs.mythic-c2.net/) is intended to serve as a plug-and-play
architecture where operators have the ability to create changes to
communication channels, agents, and other aspects as they operate. This C2

framework is the only one supporting Linux, Mac, and Windows implants
that provides flexibility for operators. Additionally, Mythic supports socks
and secure communications. Mythic is made up of a web-based frontend
utilizing React and Docker contained for the backend of the C2. Another
great feature with Mythic is the ability to map tasks executed during an
engagement to the MITRE (https://attack.mitre.org) framework.
The MITRE Framework
MITRE ATT&CK, also known as MITRE Adversarial Tactics, Techniques,
and Common Knowledge, is a publicly available knowledge base of threat
actor behaviors. These behaviors are mapped to specific tactics, techniques,
and procedures (TTPs) that any organization can utilize to have a common
language between offensive and defensive security teams. The tactic areas
include reconnaissance, resource development, initial access, execution,
persistence, privilege escalation, defense evasion, credential access,
discovery, lateral movement, collection, C2, exfiltration, and impact in the
ATT&CK matrix. The tactics areas are also broken out into 191 techniques
and 385 subtechniques. Operators can leverage the framework for
ATT&CK mappings to refine their C2 communications strategies by
understanding what TTPs will likely get picked up in an operation from
previous adversaries and how they can fill those gaps to go under the radar.
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p78_c071

### CONTENT ###
Mythic has various community profiles
(https://github.com/MythicC2Profiles) to leverage with the framework,
including the following:
• http: Utilizes GET/POST requests to the Mythic server supporting kill
dates, sleep intervals, custom headers, proxy information, and SSL support.
• dns: Leverages a DNS server listening for TXT queries from the agent for
communications supporting kill dates, sleep intervals, and a custom DNS
server.
• httpx: Is a dynamic and customizable HTTP profile. There is a range of
options that an operator can leverage, including callback domains, domain
fallback, domain rotation, encrypted key exchange, jitter, sleep intervals,
and agent messages.
• discord: Leverages Discord’s REST API for communications.
• websocket: Allows agents to connect through a static websocket
connection with support for SSL, kill dates, and sleep intervals.
• dynamichttp: Employs extendable HTTP messages with dynamic
modifications for the client and server supporting kill dates, sleep intervals,
custom headers, proxy information, and SSL support.

### CONTENT ###
C2 Communication Channels
Command and Control communications channels are required for an
operator to communicate with the C2 server. These channels are used to
send data, receive data, and maintain persistent access to an agent on a
victim’s machine. The communication methods are just as important as
your evasion tactics when delivering your payload. If you have poor
operations security (OPSEC) for your C2 channels, you will likely get
caught.
As blue teams have evolved over time, operators have had to make their
traffic blend in with existing network traffic. The most common C2
communications methods today are DNS over HTTPs and HTTP/HTTPS,
although HTTPS seems to be the top method overall.
• DNS over HTTPS (DoH) provides encryption end-to-end similarly to
using HTTPS. The DNS queries and DNS responses are sent over HTTPS.
Operators can abuse this method to reach out to DNS providers specified by
the operator, such as cloudflare-dns.com, and resolve the DNS queries all
over an encrypted channel.
• HTTP/HTTPS is legitimately used for web traffic across the Internet.
Most if not all networks will allow HTTP/HTTPS protocol communication,
which is a benefit for operators to disguise their traffic using these
protocols. From an OPSEC perspective, it is best to not use HTTP in
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p80_c073
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 80
# TOKENS: ~339
### CONTENT ###
general because the traffic will be unencrypted. The safest way to have your
traffic blend into an environment is to ensure that it is encrypted.
Redirectors
Redirectors relay traffic incoming from an external standpoint and pass the
traffic to the location specified, which could be to a host that is only
reachable through the initial redirector. Redirectors are used for C2 traffic to
hide the C2 server’s location, deceive the traffic to look legitimate, and also
protect your C2 server from being burned. The beauty of redirectors is that
even if one of your redirectors gets burned, your C2 server is safe, and you
can replace the external redirector with a new one without needing to reset
any of your C2 infrastructure.
Redirectors that will take less effort such as socat or iptables will blindly
forward traffic to your C2 server, which can be a downfall from an
operational security standpoint because Internet scanners may be able to
identify the malicious traffic. However, using more sophisticated redirectors
that leverage reverse proxies with rules helps filter down only the traffic
you want passed by leveraging specific user agents, allowing only certain
source IPs or other specific conditions to protect the infrastructure.
Redirector Types
The following are redirector types, beginning with basic methods to more
sophisticated methods:
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p81_c074
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 81
# TOKENS: ~348
### CONTENT ###
• socat is a client used to blindly forward all incoming connections, known
as “dumb pipe” traffic. The tool establishes bidirectional streams between
the specific IP and port.
• iptables is typically used as a firewall tool to control network traffic and
is a native component for Linux networking. With iptables, you can have
multiple rules and configurations for your port forwarding.
• Reverse proxies are used to protect your C2 backend by leveraging
Apache or Nginx web server configurations to forward your traffic to the
next web server specified. This helps protect the backend server’s identity
and leverage SSL certificates from an external point of view.
• Content delivery networks (CDNs) are a group of servers used to
geographically distribute content from your site to provide faster
performance for an end user. You can abuse CDNs by leveraging their
distribution addresses that will be in front of your domain. CDNs are
typically used alongside a reverse proxy setup and filter the web servers to
allow only the CDNs’ source IPs to access the external redirector.
• CDNs with reverse proxy using rewrites combine a standard reverse
proxy web server with a CDN in front of the web server and take into
account rewrites specific in the Apache/Nginx configuration. These rewrites
can control the directive and manipulate the URL on the fly based off the
configurations in place.
### END CHUNK ###

Command and Control Setup
In this section, you will learn how to set up and execute operations with the
Mythic C2. Figure 3-1 shows an example of building out infrastructure for a
typical red team.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p83_c076

# PAGE: 83
# TOKENS: ~243
### CONTENT ###
Figure 3-1 C2 Infrastructure Setup
To get started, you will need the following setup:
• Azure Ubuntu Server (B1s) to serve as the redirector
• Azure Ubuntu Server (B2ms) to serve as the C2 endpoint
• Azure CDNs for redirector domain
• Operator box (virtual machine or cloud instance)
• Windows testing box (defender disabled)
Once it is deployed, we will provision the Apache server for the domain
being used for the redirector. A blog post from Optiv’s Attack & Penetration
team (Archived at
https://lazzslayer.github.io/References/Redirectors/Redirectors_Optiv.pdf)
provides easy-to-use Apache configs that you can leverage for your server
for Apache’s site configuration. You will also need to set up the A records
for your domains being used for the infrastructure. For SSL certificate
generation, you will also need to temporarily open 80/443 to the world to
retrieve certificates for the domain, and then you can filter it back down to
allow only Azure CDN source IPs.
### END CHUNK ###

C2 Server
Prior to setting up Mythic, you should ensure that the A record for the
domain being used points to the C2 IP. For SSL certificate generation, you
will also need to temporarily open 80/443 to the world to retrieve
certificates for the domain. You will change the firewall rules after this step
to allow traffic only from your redirector IP.
Example 3-5 demonstrates how to install certificates from Let’s Encrypt for
your domain on your C2 server and move them into your Mythic directory.
The $MYTHICDIRECTORY should be set to where your Mythic directory
is stored on your C2 server.
Example 3-5 Installing Certificates for Your Domain
MYTHICDIRECTORY=(/opt/Mythic)
sudo apt-get install certbot
certbot certonly --standalone --preferred-challenges 
exampledomain.com --register-unsafely-without-email -
cp /etc/letsencrypt/live/exampledomain.com/privkey.pe
cp /etc/letsencrypt/live/exampledomain.com/fullchain.
After the certificates are generated, lock down your instance to allow only
your redirector’s IP access via HTTPS (443). Taking this step will prevent
### END CHUNK ###

scanners from directly reaching your C2. If the cloud instance where your
C2 is hosted gets identified as malicious and is reachable from the Internet,
you risk your instance being taken down by the cloud provider, along with
scanners lighting up while scanning your host.
Additionally, you can now remove your A record from your C2 domain
because the change in Example 3-5 will show the redirector server where
the C2 domain points to.
Next, you can install Mythic by using the steps shown in Example 3-6.
Example 3-6 Installing Mythic
git clone https://github.com/its-a-feature/Mythic
cd /Mythic
./install_docker_ubuntu.sh
Out of the box, Mythic does not come with any C2 profiles or agents. To
install specific agents and profiles, you can use the various options
available at the following locations:
• Mythic Agents: https://github.com/MythicAgents
• Mythic C2 Profiles: https://github.com/MythicC2Profiles

For this walkthrough, you can install the http C2 profile and the Apollo
agent, as shown in Example 3-7. We will leverage the HTTP profile because
it operates on the same protocol as the Mythic server rather than the other
profiles for the purpose of this walkthrough.
The HTTP profile includes the ability to set kill dates and sleep intervals, as
well as customize the headers, proxy information, and SSL support. The
Apollo agent is .NET based with features for dynamic command loading,
SOCKS5 support, peer-to-peer communication, unmanaged code execution,
and tons of other useful features for an operator. The Apollo agent is also
used in SpecterOps (https://specterops.io/) annual trainings. Example 3-7
and Example 3-8 demonstrate how to install your profile and agent and then
start the Mythic server.
Example 3-7 Installing the C2 Profile and Agent
sudo ./mythic-cli install github https://github.com/M
sudo ./mythic-cli install github https://github.com/M
Example 3-8 Starting Mythic
# Start Mythic Client

The C2 Profile section is customizable for the GET and POST requests. The
URIs can be customized to common requests similar to IIS servers or to
emulate another type of traffic to blend into the network. Do not use the
default options in this C2 profile section.
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p106_c095
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 106
# TOKENS: ~180
### CONTENT ###
There are multiple changes you will be making in this section, as illustrated
in Figure 3-13:
• Callback Host: The host for your traffic to call back to externally. This
will be the first hop from an external perspective.
• Callback Interval: How often your agent will call back to you in
seconds; this number is in fixed intervals.
• Callback Jitter: The jitter percentage for the callbacks of the agent; this
adds randomness to the callback to make each agent check-in vary.
• Callback Port: The port associated with your host that will be called back
to.
• Encryption Type: The encryption method used for the traffic.
• Headers: Customization of the GET/POST messages.
• Kill Date: End date for your agent to call back.
### END CHUNK ###

Note
In a real scenario, you would need to alter the C2 agent
because it’s heavily signatured and also would need to make
additional modifications to obfuscate your shellcode for
delivery to bypass protections in place for an endpoint. We
will discuss potential options to obfuscate your shellcode in
Chapter 4.

the ability to use on the victim host, as demonstrated in Figure 3-19. Feel
free to try different command options at this point and take into account
which may be safer from an OPSEC perspective and which commands are
better to stay away from for an operation.
